#6942. Bella Hadid Buys Rustic Colorado Retreat for $5.5 Million

The Victoria’s Secret angel recently started dating someone with strong family ties to the area.

The rugged allure of the Mountain West continues to attract celebrities. Tom Cruise and Oprah Winfrey both own properties in the ski mecca of Telluride. Kevin Costner glamorized multigenerational homesteading with his hit show Yellowstone and, in real life, oversees Aspen’s sprawling Dunbar Ranch. Now, model Bella Hadid—who recently opened the 2026 Victoria’s Secret runway show—just acquired an alpine retreat of her own in a remote town called Crested Butte.

Nestled deep in Colorado’s Rocky Mountains, a roughly three-hour drive from Aspen, Crested Butte has been known as the “Wildflower Capital of Colorado” since 1990, when the state legislature bestowed the honorary title on the Gunnison County town (population: 1,639).

Hadid has reportedly been living in the tiny village for “several months,” per The Wall Street Journal, which was the first to report the sale. The news also comes just days after the model went public on Instagram with new boyfriend Nathan Lacy, a construction manager who has ties to the region.

“I’ve always loved hockey living in NYC,” she wrote, alongside several snaps of her and Lacy at a Colorado Avalanche game. “And being a Colorado girl at heart, growing up here since i was young, I guess it was only a matter of time before I found my way to the @coloradoavalanche.”

Her new creekside compound hit the market over the summer for nearly $7 million with Channing Boucher of LIV Sotheby’s International Realty. Hadid reportedly got it at a discount, paying $5.5 million.

Built in 2001, the property sits on a 0.2-acre parcel with 200 feet of frontage on Coal Creek. Wrapped in weathered lumber, the three-bedroom, three-bathroom retreat was partially constructed out of two “original 100-year-old coal sheds,” per the listing. The rustic interiors, meanwhile, come with 30-foot beamed ceilings and hardwood floors reclaimed from a Virginia barn.

Rounding out the estate is a creek-front dining terrace plus a hot tub at the edge of the water.

This appears to be Hadid’s first real estate transaction since 2021, when she offloaded her New York City penthouse for $6.35 million.

#6940. The T3 Aire 360 Is The Best Multi-Styler for Jet Setters

The upgraded dual-voltage design ensures great hair days all over the world.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

I’ve gotten compliments on my hair my entire life. (I mean, I am a redhead.) But I noticed a significant uptick in flattery once I mastered the art of the bouncy at-home blowout. As a beauty editor, I’ve tried nearly every blow-dry brush and multi-styler on the market—yes, even the pricey Dyson Airwrap and beloved Shark Flexstyle. Neither of those viral tools comes close to my beloved T3 Aire 360, though.

I’m not the only one obsessed with this hair tool. When the Aire 360 first launched in 2024, my colleagues raved about how it expertly smoothed their hair, how easy it is to use, and the fact that it comes in a cute, convenient carrying pouch. T3 has since come out with an upgraded dual-voltage model that can be used all over the world. (As an avid traveler, that detail is essential!)

Could the upgraded T3 Aire 360 be the right multi-styler for you? Keep reading to discover everything you need to know about the tool and get first-person intel from two editors who can’t live without it.

T3 has all the bells and whistles you could want in a multi-styler. The tool comes with four attachments: A blow dryer, two curling wands (for each direction), and a smoothing round brush. The barrels stand out from other models because they’re ceramic, meaning they conduct heat beautifully, smooth strands, and produce impressive style longevity. (Just be careful because these can burn your hands if you touch them while using.)

Another cool feature? It’s designed with “SoftAire,” which is the dryer’s unique mechanism to help speed up drying time. The brand boasts “zero” heat damage, but obviously, there is some heat. However, it’s much cooler than a normal curling iron or hair straightener, so using this tool can protect your hair in the long run.

My hair is very fine, but I have a lot of it. It has also changed texture and become more curly over the past two years (thanks, birth control!), so I’ve had to relearn how to style and take care of it. Overall, though, I don’t have many issues with it. Stylists often say my mane is very “obedient” because it responds well to heat and is easily styled. I try to wash it two times a week and will always blow it out with a round brush.

This is a much smaller kit of attachments than I’m used to, but I actually prefer it. I don’t need all that. One of my favorite aspects is the chic leather carrying case that holds everything inside, making storage a lot easier. (So many multi-stylers don’t come with a storage bag, FYI!) Also, it’s pink. Love that.

Let me preface this by saying I’ve religiously used the T3 Aire 360 for the past two years, and the upgraded dual-voltage model functions just as beautifully. When I wash my hair at home, I blow it out with the round brush attachment. The tool is so easy and intuitive to use, and it makes my hair look incredibly smooth and sleek. I’ve perfected my at-home blowout routine with it, and most days I don’t feel the need to use a separate device—like a curling iron or flat iron—to zhuzh my hair.

When it comes to the curling wand attachments, these produce the best results out of all of the multi-stylers I’ve tested, probably because they’re ceramic. Do they get a little hot? Sure, but not at temperatures as high as your standard curling iron. The conductive ceramic material ensures my curls last for days. They might not be as defined on day two or three, but I don’t feel the need to touch them up either.

“I don’t say this lightly: My hair has never looked so good. I walked into the office with a level of confidence that should probably be studied. Who knew a good blowout could make you have a good day? The curls are so bouncy, but my ends still feel soft and not fried like a curling iron or straightener would do. And it really only took 15 minutes or so from start to finish. My Airwrap officially has steep competition. I think I’ll stick with it for my bangs, but the curling powers on the T3 Aire are unmatched.”—Beth Gillette, Beauty Editor

When it comes to multi-stylers, the T3 Aire 360 is one of the best. The price and attachments are comparable to other options, but this has one major differentiator: It’s designed for international use. The dual-voltage upgrade, along with the chic pink leather carrying case, make this the ideal choice for avid travelers. If you’re a jet setter (like me), this is the multi-styler to purchase because you’ll never experience a bad hair day, no matter where you are in the world.

#6939. What’s Your Vacation Vibe?

Whether brand new or reimagined, these four hotels offer distinctive stays. The only question is, which style to choose?

To be a discerning traveler is to have, in equal measure, preference and knowledge of the endlessly evolving hotel landscape. But even the greatest of globetrotters need a guide to what’s new every once in a while. Below, find four recently opened (or reopened) hotels, organized by travel preference: Looking for something in the city? We’ve got you. Country? We have that too. A place to hot air balloon? No worries. The toughest part will just be figuring out where to go first.

Time and Place: Perched atop the Spanish Steps, this 133-year-old mainstay is Old World luxe translated with fresh verve following a lavish update with revamped public spaces by ELLE Decor A-List designer Jacques Garcia.

Design Vibes: Delving into the archives for the Palm Court Garden redo, Garcia collaborated with Italian firm Colleoni Roberto & C. on a green-and-white-striped upholstery and with a Roman blacksmith on vintage-style lanterns.

Hide Out In… One of the damask-covered suites or at the renovated Michelin-starred Imàgo restaurant.

Time and Place: Some will recognize this property as the former home of the Standard, sans human fish tank at the check-in desk. Actually, sans any check-in desk. There’s also no room service, but there is a rooftop park with a gym, a neon blue pool, and 360° views.

Design Vibes: Legendary hotelier Ian Schrager, with his “luxury for all” philosophy, teamed up with minimalist maestro John Pawson on the architecture. Rooms feature custom-made plush seating, pale woods, and custom lighting systems for shifting moods.

Hide Out In… Your own private cineplex. Each room has a projector and 66-inch screen. Or disappear on the dance floor of the Studio, a speakeasy-style nightclub clad in marble and velvet.

Time and Place: Occupying a colonial-style former school in Old Bennington dating from 1829, this intimate inn was brought to life by Rafe Churchill, co-founder of the Elle Decor A-List firm Hendricks Churchill, and Casey Sunderland, Churchill’s partner in the new hospitality firm Place in Mind.

Design Vibes: The hotel leans into its links to a family of writers and to nearby Bennington College with an inviting library stocked with books by alums. Each of the eight unique guestrooms is cozily layered, mixing rich jewel tones and florals with antiques and art that ranges from the Hudson River School to Keith Haring.

Hide Out In… The gardens. Landscape designer Grace Fuller Marroquin lent her wild, cinematic touch to the grounds, which feature an array of native plants as well as a fruit orchard.

Time and Place: Steps from slopes that hosted ski events for two Olympics (1956 and 2026), the hotel occupies a former palazzo from the 1920s.

Design Vibes: Eschewing the traditional Alpine look, designer Achille Salvagni tailored the guestrooms with bespoke references to the local landscape and culture, like plaster bas-reliefs of mountains, ceiling motifs based on traditional clothing embroidery, and hardware in the shape of native wildlife.

Hide Out In… A hot air balloon. The hotel arranges private tours in the off season for unparalleled mountain views. Or, more down to earth, the spa includes a cryo chamber and a plunge pool.

#6935. Warren Beatty and Annette Bening’s Former L.A. Mansion Lists for Nearly $15 Million

The Hollywood power couple custom-built the gated Beverly Hills house.

The longtime Beverly Hills home of Warren Beatty and Annette Bening will soon hit the market, ELLE Decor can exclusively reveal. The married couple custom-built the gated residence in 1992 and raised their four kids there. The stars are one of Hollywood’s most famous power couples: Beatty has received 14 Academy Award nominations, winning for Best Director in 1982, and three Golden Globe Awards, while Bening has received five Oscar nominations, including Best Actress for 1999’s American Beauty, for which she won the Golden Globe.

The A-list actors parted with their longtime property in 2012, selling it for $6.7 million, per Zillow. Its new asking price will be $14.995 million.

The nine-bedroom estate’s current owners—hotelier Jonathan Baker and his wife, Jenny—acquired the property directly from Beatty and Bening, then spearheaded a $5 million renovation that concluded in 2019. They modified the Mediterranean-style home to have a more contemporary feel, with minimal moldings, glossy surfaces, modern furnishings, and bold splashes of color.

However, they still paid homage to the estate’s movie-star pedigree, creating what they call the “Warren Beatty Theater,” a 17-seat screening room in what was once Beatty’s home office.

Other nods to the legendary actor—who starred in classics like Bonnie and Clyde, Dick Tracy, and Bulworth—included integrating his drop-down projection screen into the new living room. Additional perks include a wine room with a suspended-wire storage system, a wood-paneled dressing room with plenty of built-ins, and a network of terraces.

The home’s amenities continue on the manicured grounds, which feature an infinity-edge pool, a lighted tennis court, multiple al fresco entertaining areas, and a separate guest house.

The couple are parents to four children, among them Ella Beatty, who is following in her parents’ acting footsteps and recently played Lizzie Borden in the Netflix series Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story.

The Beverly Hills house will be listed for $14.995 million with Cory Weiss of Douglas Elliman and Jacob Greene of the Altman Brothers at Douglas Elliman.

#6932. How Tiffany & Co. Made a Bird on a Rock Box

Can inspiration be infinite? Yes, if you know where to look.

Above: Tiffany & Co. Singing Bird on a Clock and Bird on a Rock brooch, 800-843-3269.

How does a yellow cockatoo flitting near an open window in Guadeloupe end up a jewelry icon? That bird knew what it was doing when it perched outside the home of Jean Schlumberger, one of Tiffany & Co’s best-known jewelry designers. Nature was always the apple of Schlumberger’s eye, and in his vision that bird would become a diamond-studded feathered friend nestled in lapis lazuli and pinned on Bunny Mellon’s beret (he would go on to make for her a full, whimsical bestiary. Theirs was a partnership of hundreds of jelly fish, camels, and seahorses, plus the beloved fruit and flowers inspired by Mellon’s own garden).

That was 1965. How to keep reaching new heights? A partnership between Tiffany & Co. and the Musée des Arts Décoratifs (MAD) in Paris offers inspiration: An archive of thousands of Schlumberger’s sketches, now being restored and digitized, reveal a mind constantly at work and a vision without boundaries. And so it continues, in spessartine and chrysoprase, in rubellites and mother-of-pearl, and turquoise. The next reincarnation of the bird, though, might be one of the most exciting horological spectacles of the year: a glimmering, singing automata atop a burst of sun, a singing gold and titanium bird on a clock. Though, we must note, it does not play music: instead, it tweets. Look, and listen.

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

#6928. It’s Time to Talk About “Duty Sex”

There are certain things that belong on your chore list: Doing laundry, washing dishes, taking the trash out, scrubbing the toilet. Sex, as you can see, is not one of those things—and it shouldn’t be. And yet, “duty sex” has become a pretty recognizable term in today’s relationships.

Duty sex typically stems from good intentions, Marisa Derr, LMFT explains. This happens when one partner wants to keep the peace or protect the relationship. It’s when you have sex because for some reason or another, you feel like you have to—not because you’re worried for your safety, but you believe that the success of your relationship lies on “fulfilling” some sexual duty.

This isn’t just an unnecessary pressure that can be demoralizing, it’s also just super unsexy.

“The problem is that sex out of obligation teaches the body that intimacy is another task, and desire fades even more,” she says. “Over time, the partner initiating feels rejected, and the partner saying yes feels used, even though neither one wants that.”

There’s an important distinction between duty sex and what we call “willing consent.” Willing consent is when you’re not necessarily feeling desire for your partner in the moment, but you consent to a sexual interaction with them because you’re open to desire showing up. It’s like when you’re not feeling particularly hungry, but you know that you really enjoy a certain meal so you go out to eat at the spot anyway. Duty sex is born from obligation, rather than curiosity—like eating that weird casserole your aunt brings on Thanksgiving when you really don’t want to, but do anyway because you prefer to not ruffle any feathers.

When did sex with our partners become that weird casserole or equivalent to scrubbing the toilet?

According to a recent study in Evolutionary Psychology, duty sex can be rooted in fear: Fear that a partner will cheat, fear that one could be less attractive than their partners and need to somehow “make up for it,” and fear that a partner will lose interest in them.

And while we typically assign duty sex as something that women mostly do—particularly because of the pressures to perform that disproportionately affect women—the study showed that men were having duty sex at similar rates as women. Though, more research is needed to find out if there is a difference between why men have duty sex and why women do it.

The thing that most people having duty sex had in common was that they were in long-term relationships.

“Couples can start worrying about how often they're having sex, rather than whether they're actually enjoying it,” sex educator Anna Richards tells Cosmopolitan. “But intimacy shouldn't be about meeting a target or keeping score. It should be something you both genuinely want.”

Sex should be about feeling pleasure and connecting with your partner in a fun, sexy, and intimate way. But for some couples, somewhere in the midst of all of the responsibilities of everyday life, sexual desire was put on the back burner. And instead of working to get it back, people are essentially forcing themselves to have duty sex to maintain their relationships. We need a serious overhaul of partnered sex!

“You can't demand desire, but you can create an environment where it has room to grow,” Richard says. Think about what the study said about why people have duty sex: they’re afraid of losing their partner. And one of the ways to bridge this gap of insecurity is to foster a relationship that comes with reassurance. If you’re the one finding yourself having duty sex, communicate with your partner about the pressure you feel. Talking about your needs—whether they come in the form of emotional security, verbal affirmations, or quality time—can help your partner learn how to help you foster desire. And if you’re the partner that’s noticing your S.O. initiate sex just to check it off a list, maybe it’s time to think about whether you’re reassuring them enough in the relationship. Do you create an environment of trust and comfort? Are you caring? Do you help them with their daily tasks?

One of the best ways to remove sex off your to-do list and onto your want-to-do list is to take it off your list completely. That may sound counterintuitive, but Richards says it helps alleviate the pressure.

“Intimacy doesn't always have to lead to the bedroom,” she says. Flirting, spending quality time together, being affectionate, making each other feel appreciated, talking about what you enjoy—these can all help rebuild a connection between you and your partner.

“Sometimes removing the expectation is exactly what allows desire to return naturally,” she says. You also need to make sure that your relationship is a place where saying “no” feels safe.

“A yes only means something when a no is fully okay,” Derr explains. “Pressure, sulking, or keeping score kills desire.”

It’s also about the little things. Saying “thank you” when they do something nice, touching each other throughout the day, complimenting each other, and just being a good partner can all lend themselves into reigniting desire.

Life is way too short to be having sex because you feel like you have to. Plus, any healthy and happy relationship would not foster an environment where sex feels like a duty in the first place. Yes, making a relationship work longterm does take work and effort—but that doesn’t mean that every aspect of your relationship should feel like work. The parts that are meant to be fueled by fun and curiosity (like sex) should remain fun. And the second sex starts feeling like a chore, it may be time to clear your checklist and make reconnecting your first priority.

#6925. Should I Be Manifesting Love?

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Sometimes, it feels like you’ve tried it all when it comes to love: downloading every dating app available, letting your friends set you up with their “nice” cousin, and even aimlessly going to a Home Depot and pretending to look confused. But when nothing seems to be working, it may be time to consider some less orthodox options, like manifesting love.

If your immediate reaction to hearing about manifestation is to roll your eyes, I get it. Manifestation can sound like a bunch of “woo-woo” for someone who doesn’t find comfort in “trusting in the universe.” But we are living in the era of the Etsy Witch, baby! And opening yourself up to love may just be on the other side of a mindset change—which is essentially all manifestation really is.

Contrary to what you might believe, manifestation is not magic. When stripped of its mystical claims, the practice is grounded in neuroscience, psychology, and behavioral alignment.

A 2025 study showed that people who more strongly believed in manifestation tended to perceive themselves as more successful, have bigger aspirations, and believe they were more likely to achieve future success. And because they believed these things about themselves, they were more likely to go after their goals and take bigger risks—which can all translate to your love life.

“Manifestation gives people hope, especially after heartbreak and reminds them that their past experiences don't have to determine their future in love,” Noah, psychic advisor at California Psychics tells Cosmopolitan.

So you can’t exactly just light a candle, declare “I want love” out loud and then move on with your day—that’s not exactly how manifestation works. In practice, it takes a lot of thought about what it is you truly want out of a relationship.

“Manifesting love is about getting clear on the kind of relationship you want, not just the person you hope to attract,” Noah says. “I encourage people to think about how they want to feel in a relationship, whether that's emotionally secure, appreciated, respected or genuinely loved.” Sometimes, doing things like visualizing, saying affirmations, meditating, and journaling—all things you’d be advised by a therapist—can help you figure out what it is you’re looking for.

And then you have to take stock of your life and your attitude. If you’re someone who’s been closed off to romance (totally fair), it’ll be hard to let something new and exciting in your life.

“You have to be willing to make room for the kind of love you're asking for,” Noah says.

You also have to be careful to not mix up manifestation with wishful thinking. If you’re already into someone or dating them and want them to be “the one,” manifestation isn’t really going to do much to change someone else’s behaviors.

Noah explains that healthy manifestation should bring you a sense of peace, confidence, and clarity. Wishful thinking, on the other hand, can keep you attached to a situation that isn't fulfilling your needs. This is why it’s important to really understand your situation before you try to manifest love out of it.

“Manifestation should never require you to ignore reality or compromise your self worth,” Noah says.

Plus, manifesting a specific person rather than the kind of relationship you want can end up being counterproductive to your love life. Noah says that when someone becomes hyperfocused on manifesting one specific person, they sometimes close themselves off to other possibilities. And manifestation won’t make someone love you. Love involves two people and everyone has their own free will.

“I don't believe manifestation should become an excuse to wait indefinitely for someone who isn't showing up for you,” he says. “Sometimes releasing that attachment is what allows you to become open to something healthier.”

But of course, any kind of love—manifested or otherwise—requires you to feed into yourself, first and foremost. And manifestation can play a role in self love, too.

Noah explains that taking the time to understand what you want can help better inform what you look for (and ask the universe for). You need to start with the relationship with yourself.

“Set your intentions, trust your intuition, and remain open to how love might enter your life without trying to control every detail,” Noah says. “And when someone does come along, pay attention to their actions because chemistry may bring two people together but consistency, communication, and mutual respect are what help love last.”

And once you’ve successfully manifested the love of your life, you can use positive thinking and reinforcement—AKA manifestation—to show up better everyday in your relationship.

A study as old as 1996 found that couples who had more positive perceptions of each other were more likely to stay together, experience increases in relationship satisfaction, and report fewer conflicts and doubts. By manifesting the type of person you want to be in a relationship (while actually in it), your mind will work to make it a reality.

Unfortunately, there is no secret method to landing the love you want. But if changing your outlook on things and keeping your mind open might help you find what you’re looking for, why not try?

#6924. The Breakfast at Tiffany’s NYC Townhouse Sells for $11.5 Million

The historic residence traded at a $3.5 million discount.

Holly Golightly might have had questionable taste in men, but she had a keen eye for real estate. Memorably portrayed by Audrey Hepburn in Breakfast at Tiffany’s, Golightly lived in a handsome Upper East Side brownstone that had been subdivided into apartments. Though the 1961 classic film shot its interior scenes on a Los Angeles soundstage, the exterior of the New York City townhouse served as a backdrop throughout the movie.

In May 2025, the five-story residence hit the market for $15 million, following a total overhaul that included a slew of luxe upgrades, such as a new roof terrace and an elevator. Although StreetEasy listed the property as “in contract” last December, its official sale date is November 11, 2026. In the end, the two-family house traded hands for $11.5 million, roughly 23 percent off the original price, per Realtor.com.

Set on a tree-lined block between Lexington and Third Avenues, the East 73rd Street home is distinguished by its Tiffany Blue front door and matching window casings. Built in 1866 by architect John Sexton, the Italianate-style landmark encompasses nearly 4,500 square feet and has four bedrooms in total, three in the main residence—which spans the top four floors—and one in the garden-level apartment, which has its own separate entrance.

The seller was retired beverage executive Joseph Harkins, who acquired the property in 2015 for $7.4 million and spearheaded a complete transformation that modernized the interiors while still preserving the house’s 19th-century charm. The project even included excavating the basement level, which now has nearly eight-foot ceilings, a powder room, a second laundry room, and a wine cellar.

Outside, the 470-square-foot rear garden is fully enclosed by Walpole lattice fencing and sports stonework paving. It will no doubt be the scene of plenty of “Breakfasts” for the home’s newest steward.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6917. I Just Finished My Kitchen Renovation, and This “Hidden” Island Feature Is the One Upgrade I’d Recommend to Everyone

It’s made my cook space so much more functional.

I’m always noticing something cool in someone else’s home and thinking, “Oh, I need that, too.” That happened last year when my family stayed with my in-laws. It was my first time seeing their newly renovated kitchen, and after a quick tour, one small detail caught my eye: the pop-up outlet in the kitchen island.

It was simple, practical, and exactly the kind of feature I wanted in my own kitchen. When we recently renovated our cook space, it went to the top of our wish list—and it has indeed been a game-changer with how we use the area.

Ahead, here’s why I love having a pop-up outlet, what to consider before installing one, and a few other kitchen gadgets that you might want to consider adding to your next upgrade.

Like the name suggests, a pop-up outlet is basically an electrical outlet that hides inside your countertop until you need it. Most models have a top you press or pull, and the outlet rises up so you can plug in your blender, mixer, or whatever else you're using. When you're done, you push it back down and it's out of sight.

There are a few different versions, including ones that pop up manually and others that rise with the push of a button. Some, like the one we have, include USB ports or wireless charging built in. The outlet connects to power underneath the countertop, so it's something you'll want to plan for before your new counters go in.

Like pretty much any kitchen gadget, there are a range of options available from budget to high end. We opted for something mid-tier that still included the main functions we wanted like the additional USB ports and the charging capability on the top. If you’re a chronic “let your phone get down to one or two percent before finally charging it” person like me, I highly recommend this.

There also are some that will go nearly fully flush with the countertop, and can even have part of the countertop material on the top of it to look seamless. We did not go this route, but if you prefer the look of a flawless countertop, this might be the way to go.

We put ours in the corner of our kitchen where it’s almost entirely hidden by decor. That corner of the kitchen was otherwise without any spots to plug in appliances, so this fixed multiple problems by having electricity access while remaining discreet.

You can really put them anywhere you want, but keep in mind where you’ll need extra power accessibility and where it won’t be an eyesore, if that’s a concern. I’m also a big fan of in-drawer charging outlets.

There was some back-and-forth about adding this pop-up outlet to our kitchen remodel budget. The outlet itself isn’t break-the-bank expensive, but there were extra expenses that went with it, like hiring an electrician and paying to have a hole cut in the new countertop.

Though we decided it was worth the expense, here are some of the pros and cons you should consider if you’re on the fence.

I’m always so impressed by kitchens that integrate small, helpful gadgets like this one into their design. Here are a few other popular additions that we either made or were thinking about adding to our renovation.

Maybe it’s just a me thing, but I feel like the harder it is to find an appliance or trash can, the fancier a kitchen is. The pull-out trash can was integrated into our kitchen peninsula, and we purchased a more expensive panel-ready dishwasher so it looked like a regular cabinet. An integrated refrigerator was almost twice as expensive as a regular one, so we did not go that route.

With the way our kitchen is laid out, we unfortunately have two awkward corner cabinets. For one, we upgraded with blind corner cabinet hardware, which was well worth it.

We did not integrate a bottle rinser into our current kitchen design, but we did in our last two kitchens and loved them. It got so much use when our two kids were babies, since we could quickly rinse out bottles. They’re also great for wine drinkers or anyone who hosts a lot of parties.

Admittedly, this one is controversial, as some designers don’t like this feature. But personally, we love the look and it’s next on our “cool kitchen wish list.”

He has written for multiple home improvement magazines and websites, drawing on his own experience renovating homes with his wife to create practical, inspiring content for homeowners and DIY enthusiasts alike.

#6916. Helen Mirren’s New “Split” Bixie Is the Most Sophisticated Short Style for Women Over 50: How to Recreate It

This cut is both versatile and sophisticated

Finding a short hairstyle that is both modern and timeless isn't easy, but Dame Helen Mirren has mastered it. Her softly swept bixie, complete with a side fringe, is a particularly elegant example.

The actor debuted her asymmetric cut, coined the “split” bixie, at the London premiere of her upcoming film, A Talent for Murder. Defined by its pixie-like proportions and bob-length, face-framing front, the bixie is gaining ground as one of this year's most popular haircuts.

"A bixie has all the benefits of a short style, but because the front is kept longer and softer, it has none of the severity of other short haircuts," Sasha Leslie, senior hairstylist at Nicola Clarke, previously told Harper’s Bazaar U.K.. The cut also adds versatility, as the longer front sections can be swept to the side, worn loose around the face, or styled back for a more polished finish.

For the occasion, Mirren opted for a split bixie cut, a more directional version of the classic style, with a deep side parting and a sweeping fringe that frames the face. Of course, age shouldn't dictate your haircut, but for those with mature hair looking for a sophisticated, easy-to-style length, the split bixie makes a compelling case.

If you're tempted to follow suit, consider a few details before your next salon appointment. As Leslie notes, the shorter length does require regular trims to maintain its shape. However, if you're too busy to schedule frequent appointments, she recommends keeping the bixie slightly longer, closer to a bob, so it grows out seamlessly. For thicker hair, she suggests asking your hairstylist to remove excess bulk while keeping length; thinner textures benefit from a more graduated silhouette to create the illusion of extra volume.

When it comes to styling, the split bixie's key feature is its side-swept part. It adds softness and movement to the shorter cut and is also a clever trick to achieve a natural contour. "Wearing a sleek side parting is a great way for one's hairstyle to contribute to a sharper, more contoured appearance, by adding more angles and height to the face," trend forecaster and hairstylist Tom Smith previously told Harper’s Bazaar U.K.. "Most people will suit a side parting more on one side than the other, so it's a good idea to try out a side parting on either side of your face to see which is more flattering for you."

Above all, Mirren's look reminds us that great hair has no age limit. With its flattering shape, soft movement, and effortless appeal, the bixie makes a strong case for embracing shorter lengths at any stage of life.

#6913. 5 Ways Warmth Has Shaped Interior Design in 2026, According to Experts

Warmth is more than a palette—it’s the secret to a home you never want to leave.

After years of cool neutrals and minimalist interiors, there’s been a shift toward warmer, more inviting spaces that feel layered, personal, and lived-in.

“Warmth has become more than just a color palette or a particular material. It's about spaces that feel authentic, comfortable, and reflective of the people who live in them,” says Laura Gerber, founder of ODE Design.

At the end of the day, a warm home should feel inviting and effortless, with materials and details that make people want to spend time there. If you’re looking to add some warmth to your home, take inspiration from these designer-approved ideas.

Wood’s organic grain brings instant warmth to both modern and traditional homes, adding depth without making a space feel overly formal.

“We’re incorporating more white oak, walnut, and rift-cut cabinetry than we have in years, along with wood ceilings and paneled walls,” explains Rachel Simpson, senior interior designer at Revive Design and Renovation.

There’s been a shift from stark white countertops toward natural quartzite, travertine, and marble with warmer veining. These materials have natural variation and movement that bring character into a space.

“Honed finishes, in particular, feel softer and more organic than high-polish surfaces. They allow the beauty of the stone to speak for itself without feeling overly formal or glossy,” explains Simpson.

These days, people really want their homes to feel like a sanctuary.

“In a recent project, the client envisioned a grown-up treehouse nestled among the evergreens. We wanted to create a warm yet contemporary mountain retreat that felt luxurious but also comfortable and welcoming,” says Charles Greenfeld, founder and principal designer at Ethan Charles Design.

Rather than relying on one or two dramatic statement pieces, the interiors were designed to be experienced as a whole, with each detail contributing to that warm aesthetic.

Creating warmth is really about creating layers of thoughtfully curated design elements. This helps to create a home you want to enter and never leave.

“Well-proportioned rugs, textures on walls, loads of art, draperies, and lighting overhead and at eye level are just some of the things we can add to our spaces to make them feel cozy and accessible,” says Ashley Powell, cofounder and interior designer of Roseberry Allen.

Designers have lately been drawn to rooms where the colors are close to one another, but the materials do different things.

“A woven rug, a textured wall, and a piece of wood with a visible grain each catch the light differently,” says Jess Klein, founder and principal of Jess Klein Studio.

That variation adds visual depth and makes the room feel warmer and more inviting, even when the color palette is understated.

Eschewing conventional design for an immersive, romantic kitchen, this wildly glamorous space proves that high-tech convenience can foster intimacy, slowness, and the art of living well.

#6906. Meet ‘The Altruists’ Cast and Characters

You know a true story hates to see them coming.

The fact that the characters of Netflix’s The Altruists feel like one big family is what the computer science community would call a bug, not a feature. Based on real people and led by two true story mavens, Julia Garner and Anthony Boyle, the series documents how one of the many reasons the collapse of the cryptocurrency exchange company FTX happened is that the employees were all friends, lovers, and emotionally tangled in ways that made it hard to do business ethically. That and the blatant fraud. As you get sucked into the series, and the many romances at the center of all that crime, let me provide you with a cheat sheet for the cast and characters of The Altruists.

Most of the actors on the Netflix series are young, because the people they are portraying were young when this all went down. The adult cast includes some pretty recognizable names, like Jennifer Grey (Dirty Dancing), as well as character actors like Robin Weigart (Deadwood), Paul Reiser (Mad About You), and William Mapother (Lost). But let’s get to know the kids’ table, because unfortunately they were and still are running this world even after the events of The Altruists.

Caroline is the co-CEO of Alameda Research and Sam’s on/off girlfriend.

Garner previously played Anna Delvey, a very different type of scammer, in the miniseries Inventing Anna. But she’s best known for playing Ruth on Ozark for four seasons. The role earned her a Golden Globe and three Emmy Awards. Prior to that, she had a recurring role on The Americans. Her other television credits include Maniac, Dirty John, Waco and The Get Down. She played the villain in an MCU movie last year, Fantastic Four: First Steps, NBD. Some of her other film credits include Weapons, Wolf Man, The Assistant, and Martha Marcy May Marlene.

Sam is the CEO of FTX, and Caroline’s on/off girlfriend.

Since originating the role of Scorpius Malfoy in Harry Potter and the Cursed Child, for which he won a freakin’ Olivier Award, Boyle has been dominating the based-on-a-true-story genre. On television, that includes: House of Guinness, Say Nothing, Manhunt, and Masters of the Air. In film, that includes Tetris, Danny Boy, and Tolkien. You’ve also seen him in Shardlake, The Plot Against America, Ordeal by Innocence and two episodes of Derry Girls.

Ryan became the CEO of FTX Digital Markets after working at Alameda Reserch.

Rife is a standup comedian with a smattering of acting experiences, including shows like Gamer's Guide to Pretty Much Everything, Fresh Off The Boat, That ‘90s Show, and the Scrubs reboot. You’ve also seen him in films like The Curse of Wolf Mountain and I Live Here Now.

Hu is a former child actress you know from Best Friends Whenever and Bizaardvark. More recently, her credits include The Brothers Sun, Man on the Inside, and Rooster. She has also appeared in several thriller films, like Voyagers, The Boogeyman, and Night Shift.

#6903. The One Kitchen Feature Designers Always Splurge on—and Why It’s Worth It

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: Meghan Jay selected a brass chandelier with ribbed glass orbs to modernize this traditional kitchen.

The kitchen is the hardest-working room in your house, but it’s also one of the most expensive spaces to renovate. Depending on the size and scope of your redo, reimagining this space can cost up to six figures, so it’s important to be wise about allocating your budget. While some people may overspend on high-end appliances or really luxurious countertops, you should never overlook the room’s lighting. Turns out, that’s where designers always splurge in the kitchen.

“Lighting has such a powerful ability to change how a room feels,” explains Meghan Jay, an interior designer in Winnetka, Illinois. “You can have the most beautiful cabinetry, stone, and hardware, but if the lighting isn't right, the space can feel flat or even cold.”

Meghan Meehan of Hudson East Design in New Jersey agrees and says the right fixtures can provide height, dimensionality, and lots of mood to the space. “Don't think of lighting as the finishing touch,” she adds. “Think of it as one of the main design elements that makes the entire kitchen.”

Ahead, here’s why spending a little more on your kitchen lighting is worth it—and exactly how much you should budget for.

Every kitchen deserves to glow at full wattage, but it will cost you. After all, illuminating a kitchen isn’t as simple as adding a budget-friendly portable lamp and calling it a day: The best and brightest kitchens usually include several sources such as sconces, recessed ceiling fixtures, and under-cabinet lighting to illuminate meal-prepping zones.

“It needs to provide the right illumination for cooking and everyday tasks, but it also creates warmth and ambiance when you're entertaining or gathering with family,” Meehan explains. As a rule of thumb, she recommends homeowners allocate at least 8 to 12 percent of their kitchen renovation budget to lighting.

That said, anyone who wants to wow with some statement fixtures might wind up spending more. Jay, for example, suggests setting aside $3,000 to $7,000 for decorative wares such as pendant lights above an island.

Admittedly, spending thousands of dollars on lighting is bound to elicit some sticker shock, and some people may wonder if it’s worth it. However, Lauren Wodicka insists it’s worth the investment. “Unlike a sofa, rug, or an appliance, lighting doesn’t experience the same daily wear and tear,” the Northern Virginia designer reasons. “A beautiful, well-made fixture can last for generations and become the focal point of a room.”

Oftentimes, less expensive fixtures can read cheap. “You may notice overly shiny or artificial-looking brass finishes, visible seams, thin metal, or cheaper-looking glass,” Jay says. “Higher-quality fixtures often have more substantial materials, beautiful patinas, and thoughtful details that become more apparent the closer you are.”

If you want to get the best bang for your buck, devise a shopping strategy. Santa Monica-based designer Christine Markatos Lowe first selects functional lighting and then spends the rest of her budget on “decorative pieces with personality.” But make no mistake, a personable light fixture should still have staying power: Lowe often reaches for timeless silhouettes from retailers like Urban Electric and Ann-Morris Lighting and is a sucker for details like handblown glass or “beautifully finished metals.”

“The key to finding the right balance between statement lighting and a cohesive kitchen design is to make sure every element feels intentional,” Lowe explains. “It’s all about allowing each material or element to have its moment without overwhelming the space.”Wodicka—who calls Soane, Hudson Valley Lighting, and Visual Comfort her go-tos—says less is more. “Not every kitchen island needs two or three lighting pendants,” she explains. “Sometimes one beautiful fixture can make a stronger statement.”

Designers often say lighting is the jewelry of a room, so layering different materials, sizes, and finishes adds more visual intrigue. Wodicka wants kitchen lights to complement each other—and the fixtures in surrounding rooms—but they don’t have to be identical. “A light fixture doesn't necessarily have to match the cabinet hardware or plumbing fixtures,” Jay adds. “I often prefer mixing finishes to create a more collected, layered look.”

If you want to keep your fixtures cohesive—without identical finishes—stick with a particular style. For example, a sconce with pleated fabric shades will look out of place next to a midcentury-style pendant that looks like it belongs on the set of Mad Men.

For the perfect finishing touch, Meehan recommends putting all lights on dimmers, which is a simple detail that can make a huge difference. “This allows for functional lighting when cooking, but softer light to warm the space when you are entertaining,” she adds. And, if a small detail can make your investment work even harder, even better.

#6900. How Noah Cyrus’s Deep Connections to ‘The Hunger Games’ Led to Her Chilling “Ash and Snow” Music Video for ‘Sunrise on the Reaping’ (Exclusive)

The singer-songwriter says she “absolutely manifested” being part of The Hunger Games universe.

Noah Cyrus knows ball when it comes to The Hunger Games. So it’s no surprise that the country crooner lent her richly smooth vocals and deeply confessional songwriting chops to the soundtrack of the latest film in the franchise, Sunrise on the Reaping.

When her song, “Ash and Snow,” was officially announced as part of the soundtrack—which, like those of Hunger Games past, includes undeniable bangers—her fans celebrated how their manifestations came true. It turns out Noah had long dreamed of contributing to Suzanne Collins’s universe, too.

“I absolutely manifested this!” Noah exclusively tells Cosmopolitan. “I have watched the movies millions of times. These are films I go back to over and over again. I watch them multiple times a year. I feel like I’ve been holding in the words, just ready for the chance to contribute in my own way.”

“Ash and Snow” is a chilling track that calls back to President Snow’s cruelty in the Second Quarter Quell we see pan out in the latest installment—but the video mostly contains visuals inspired by the OG trilogy’s heroine, Katniss Everdeen.

“Throughout the process, I was thinking of Katniss and President Snow and her courage in leading a rebellion against the Capitol and Snow,” Noah says, adding that she had written the track in under an hour. She explains that “Ash and Snow” isn’t only connected to one film but the whole Hunger Games franchise.

“I spoke with the studio about the plot and all the imagery, and the words just came flooding in. It was something I felt in my body. I knew what I wanted to say and write,” she continues. “There has been a lot in my life where I feel like I’ve had to sacrifice parts of myself, and I’ve had times where I wanted to seek vengeance when I’ve felt wronged or hurt. This song embodies strength, but it also comes from a place of pain and seeking justice.”

Luckily, Noah gave Cosmo the first look at her new music video for “Ash and Snow.” Come for the achingly beautiful visuals, and stay for the vengeance, justice, and sacrifice that shaped the song.

#6899. The “Scottish Country House” Look Is the Cozy Interior Trend Designers Say to Know This Season

You don't need a Highland cottage to nail this heritage-inspired look.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

You don't need a sprawling stone cottage in the Highlands, a sweeping countryside view, or a roaring fireplace to bring a little Scottish country-house charm into your home. In fact, the appeal of this cozy, heritage-inspired aesthetic is that it can work just as beautifully in a city apartment as it does in a centuries-old estate.

The secret is mixing those old-world details with pieces that feel fresh and livable, so your home doesn't end up looking like a period drama set. A tartan runner on the dining table, a cashmere throw draped over your favorite reading chair, or a few leather accents on the coffee table can bring just the right amount of countryside character to an otherwise modern space. Strathberry's new tartan homeware collection is another lovely entry point, giving the traditional pattern a contemporary spin that feels right at home in all kinds of interiors.

Whether you're fully embracing your countryside daydreams or just looking to make your space feel a little warmer and more layered this winter, there are plenty of ways to bring the look home. Read on for the Scottish country-house-inspired touches that make this aesthetic so charming—and how to work them into your own space.

If there's one pattern that deserves a starring role in a Scottish country-inspired home, it's tartan. And you don't have to limit it to a throw blanket or a few accent pillows. Designer Kaitlin Smith recommends “using plaid for a wallpaper or even as an accent pillow” to bring that distinctly Scottish feel into a space.

For a bolder take, try a tartan wallpaper in a powder room, entryway, or tucked-away reading nook. If you'd rather ease into the look, a single plaid pillow or upholstered accent piece can make just as much of a statement. With so many different tartan patterns and colorways to choose from, you can find one that brings a little heritage-inspired character to your home without making the whole room feel like a theme.

A Scottish country house should feel like it's been lived in for generations, not styled within an inch of its life. That's where woven baskets come in.

Noel Gatts, a designer and HGTV personality, recommends introducing “some slouchy woven baskets” throughout the home, whether at the entryway for shoes and umbrellas or in a living room or bedroom to hold extra blankets. Their slightly relaxed shape and natural texture add to that collected, lived-in feeling while giving everyday clutter somewhere to go.

Try a larger basket beside the front door, a smaller one tucked under a console or an oversized version next to your favorite reading chair. It's a practical detail that also brings in the natural materials that make this aesthetic feel so grounded. Plus, having a cozy throw within arm's reach is never a bad idea.

You can practically picture a pot of tea warming by the fire in a Scottish country house, and Gatts recommends leaning into that feeling with a thoughtful arrangement of crockery and candles.

“Feature a fireplace or dining table with tea pots, earthenware, and an installation of candles and/or candlesticks,” she suggests. This is a lovely opportunity to bring out those pieces you might otherwise save for special occasions, from vintage teapots and handmade ceramic bowls to mismatched candlesticks and well-loved serving dishes. Grouping them together creates a charming focal point without making the space feel overly formal.

Candlelight is especially important here, adding a soft, welcoming glow that feels worlds away from bright overhead lighting. And if you don't have a fireplace, a dining table, mantel or console can give you the same cozy effect.

A Scottish country house shouldn't feel too coordinated, so don't be afraid to bring a few different patterns into the mix. As designer Sarah Cole suggests, “try pairing a wool plaid with a botanical print, a small geometric pattern or a traditional rug.”

The combination adds visual interest and gives a room that layered, lived-in quality that makes country-house interiors so inviting. You can keep the look cohesive by pulling a few warm, autumnal colors throughout the patterns—like russets, burgundies, forest greens and ochres—while varying their scale so they don't compete with one another. It's a simple way to make the space feel more personal and collected over time.

The Scottish country-house look has a wonderful appreciation for pieces that feel like they've been around awhile. Rather than filling every surface with brand-new accessories, Gatts suggests bringing in “found objects with a sense of history” made from weathered wood, pottery, or patinated brass. These are the kinds of details that give a room its personality, whether it's an old wooden box on a coffee table, a vintage ceramic vessel or a brass object with a little wear and patina.

Don't worry about everything matching, either. A mix of materials and finishes makes the space feel more authentic and less like you ordered the entire room in one shopping trip. Gatts recommends using these pieces on a coffee table for “the perfect antique flair,” but a bookshelf, sideboard, or entryway console would work just as well.

Finally, a few well-chosen antique details can give a room that Highland lodge feeling without requiring a full redesign. Jill Wheeler of Birch Hill Interiors recommends adding “a touch of polished brass like antique candlesticks or wall sconces to give the space a refined look.” Try styling a pair of vintage brass candlesticks on a mantel, placing one on a coffee table, or swapping in brass wall sconces beside a favorite reading chair.

Wheeler also suggests incorporating antiques on a mantel, coffee table, or built-in, noting that “antiques can help bring the outside in and tell a story.” The mix of aged finishes and meaningful finds adds a sense of history and character that makes a Scottish country-inspired space feel genuinely lived in.

#6895. High-Gloss Furniture Will Be Everywhere in 2027—Here's How Designers Make It Feel Fresh

A single lacquered piece can make a neutral room feel instantly more interesting.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

After years of quiet luxury, soft neutrals, and interiors that favor understated details, a little drama feels like a very welcome change. High-gloss furniture is making a comeback, bringing shine, color, and a bit of personality back into our homes. Whether it's a lacquered accent table, a sculptural console, or a statement cabinet with a reflective finish, these pieces have a way of catching your eye and making even the simplest room feel more interesting. And honestly, who says every piece of furniture has to blend into the background?

The look also ties into Neo Deco, a modern take on Art Deco that brings together glamorous materials, bold silhouettes, and a fresh approach to decorating. But you don't need to turn your living room into a scene from The Great Gatsby to get in on it—a single glossy piece can add just the right amount of contrast to a neutral space, while mixing lacquer with natural wood, textured upholstery, or warm metallic accents keeps things feeling layered and livable.

Ahead, designers share how to bring high-gloss furniture into your home and embrace a little more shine without going over the top.

Not ready to go all-in on the high-gloss trend? An accent table is your low-commitment, high-impact way in. Tucked beside your favorite armchair or pulled up next to the sofa, a lacquered side table brings just enough shine to make the whole corner feel a little more interesting. The best part is the contrast, so have fun mixing finishes. Carol Alda, interior designer at Z Gallerie, loves pairing a glossy table with a “velvet or textured woven chair.” Just make sure the table sits at a comfortable height next to the chair so you can actually use it for your coffee (or cocktail).

Color is another way to make the piece feel right at home. Ali Esmaili, design expert and owner of Esmaili Rugs, suggests pulling the shade of your accent table from a patterned rug, which creates an easy connection between the two. An oxblood, emerald, or classic black table brings plenty of Neo Deco attitude, especially in a sculptural shape. It's a small swap, but one that can give even the most neutral living room a welcome dose of personality.

A little high-gloss feels right at home in a bar or entertaining area, where a touch of drama is practically part of the assignment. A lacquered bar credenza or sideboard in a saturated shade can turn your glassware and favorite bottles into a whole display moment.

Jennifer Fitzpatrick, founder of Studio J & Co., explains that high-gloss finishes bring extra energy to a room by reflecting daylight and catching the flicker of candles and lamps in the evening. In a bar area, that means your decanters, glassware, and cocktail accessories get their own little spotlight. Fitzpatrick recommends pairing high-gloss pieces with unlacquered brass, fluted glass, and textured textiles.

The coffee table is already the unofficial centerpiece of the living room, so why not give it a little extra attention? A lacquered version in a rich color or an unexpected shape can instantly make the space feel more interesting. And don't worry about all that shine feeling too formal. Fitzpatrick says today's high-gloss pieces are all about sculptural shapes, from curved profiles to clean geometric forms. A lacquered cocktail table can also catch the glow of your lamps in the evening, adding a little atmosphere without visually crowding your seating arrangement.

To keep the look feeling relaxed, mix that reflective surface with pieces that have plenty of texture. Esmaili loves pairing high-gloss furniture with vintage rugs, noting that “the gloss adds depth, movement and a touch of theatre.” The contrast between a shiny coffee table and a well-loved rug with plenty of pattern and texture gives the room a more layered, lived-in feel. Add a soft sectional and a few simple accessories, and you've got a statement piece that still feels right at home.

#6893. The 31 Best Gifts for the Snobbiest People You Know

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We found the stuff they don’t already own.

For the audiophile whose vinyl collection rivals that of an actual record store, a Fluance turntable will do their records justice. As a fragrance expert who tests new scents for a living, few earn me as many compliments as Althair from Parfums de Marly. It’s a grown-up vanilla, with notes of orange blossom, cinnamon, and a dose of smoky guaiac wood to keep it from going saccharine. Although it’s technically from the men’s line, its not-too-sweet gourmand scent profile is truly genderless.

The iRestore red light face mask just won a 2026 Gadget Award. Enough said.

Their parched, damaged strands deserve nothing but the best; in this case, it’s Kérastase’s Oléo-Relax hair mask. It’s got a ton of hydrating ingredients—rose oil, coconut oil, and shorea oil—to replenish dry hair. Not to mention, it smells damn good.

#6888. Why Everything New Looks a Little Vintage Right Now

Designers are adding depth to their ensembles by mixing and matching eras with irreverence

Backstage at the Spring 2027 Chanel show in Paris, after a parade of elegantly skirt-suited women strolled beneath an extravagant set of birds suspended midflight, Matthieu Blazy told a story of picking his teenage nephew up from school. Observing the students, he was struck by the irreverent way they dressed. “They wear a lot of vintage. They are not dictated by a decade—they are a lot more free,” he told Bazaar. “They wear something from the ’30s, ’50s, ’70s. I wanted to expand on that.”

One look in particular felt more akin to how someone would dress to attend the Chanel show rather than to walk it—an old-school Chanel jacket worn with new Chanel shoes, a pair of black trousers, eclectic jewelry and an old belt. It wasn’t an anomalous look; the vintagey vibes loomed large. Knee-length skirt suits were styled with heaps of necklaces. Men’s shirting was mixed with relaxed tailoring and leather separates; tiny white tank tops peeked from beneath tweed jackets festooned with oversized, floral brooches.

Loving and collecting vintage (or better yet, holding onto pieces until they became vintage) was once the domain of fashion insiders. But today, young people are growing up native to the secondhand market. (According to ThredUp’s 2026 Resale Report, 62 percent of Gen Z shopped secondhand last year.) As they age, dressing from a closet that contains clothing from multiple style eras has become inevitable. And designers are taking notice.

Just as the Dolce & Gabbana show was about to start in Milan a week before the Chanel show, the loudspeaker turned on to regale the audience on “the wardrobes of Sicily’s grand houses. Clothes once worn by great-grandmothers, grandmothers, mothers, aunts and daughters. A slip dress is worn over a jacket, antique lace meets contemporary pieces, lingerie and eveningwear coexist, and the precious and the everyday merge in an unexpected balance.” The styling made it feel as if a young woman pilfered grandma’s closet, mixing an antique, pussy bow blouse with embellished and ripped jeans or pairing a D&G baby tee with a leopard-trimmed, tweedy jacket.

At Gucci, Demna dissected the archetypes of the bourgeoisie, including “the rebellious aristocrat.” A series of looks paired variations of rugby shirts, pleated tartan skirts, puff-shaped bomber jackets with oversized belts adorned with horsebits and heaps of jewelry piled onto necklines. It’s what a “blue-blooded child of an aristocrat might wear to the private school that she hates,” Demna mused after the show. Within that layered ensemble, he defined a character: Her kilt is part of her school uniform; her rugby polo and leather jacket were mined from a thrift store or her dad’s closet. She wore all these pieces together with no regard for where or what era they came from.

The idea of dressing “vintage” on a runway is inherently contradictory. The runways are a place of new; vintage is, by definition, old. But the concept is more about adding depth and texture to an ensemble than it is about the age of the garment itself. It’s not about, as in seasons past, garments that are distressed to look worn and weathered. It’s about achieving a kind of idiosyncrasy in a landscape that had become samey and polished and “quiet luxury.” It’s about a desire for irreverence.

The Row is a spectacular case study here, particularly as it was one of the brands that ushered in the minimalist look of the early 2020s. So how to evolve without alienating your customer? For Spring 2027, the Olsen twins draped and knotted antique linens, lace, and doilies over their typical rotation of sleek, black trousers and perfectly mussed trench coats. Models wore layers of dainty beads or single chunky necklaces that nodded subtly towards eclecticism or bohemian.

Fashion must contend and engage with vintage; increasingly, it is just a part of how people shop. As the secondhand market gains traction, it presents new challenges; houses are in constant competition with their own archives. Just look at the resurgences of secondhand Pucci and Etro from the early 2000s. Many have embraced reissues: Chloé’s Maxime wedge or the McQueen skull scarf, for example, shown tucked into contemporary collections of recent years. Of course, creative directors at legacy houses have always had to find ways to be in conversation with their archives and predecessors. The greatest designers, however, manage to use this formula to their advantage.

Take Blazy at Chanel, for instance. He must contend with Gabrielle “Coco” Chanel’s founding legacy of the early and mid-1900s in addition to Karl Lagerfeld’s from the ’80s through the 2010s. The idea of a knee-length skirt suit in nubby fabric could seem retro, but Blazy leaned into it instead of pushing against it. He didn’t rewrite the idea entirely, but shifted our perception with subtle tweaks in materials, textures, and styling. He even sent Gabrielle’s original 2.55 bag down the runway, paired with a gauzy white cocktail dress and then again later with a black skirt suit.

Ralph Lauren mined his own greatest hits for a spring collection that was a glorious mishmash of old and new, clashing Americana Westernwear and prep with tropes of the English aristocracy, and even utilitarian workwear. He strung models’ waistcoats with pocket watches, paired brocade evening jackets with distressed denim, and matched ruffled-collar blouses with metallic skirts.

It’s that dialogue between items that conjures the feel of someone who wears vintage, someone who curates their closet with stylistic depth, nuance, and contrast. What young people achieve with aplomb is decidedly difficult to manufacture. But that’s what makes it so desirable. It turns runway models from mannequins into characters and makes you want to learn their stories.

#6885. It’s the Season of the Statement Glove

Vivid colors, lush leathers, and fingerless styles: Gloves have never been bolder.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

If the late, great, legendary fashion editor Diana Vreeland were still alive, I believe she would incorporate a question like, “Why don’t you replace your ring stacks with statement gloves?” into her famous “Why don’t you” column, where she rhetorically mused on ridiculous and often opulent situations. A good glove is a winter staple, yes. But lately, fashion has been having a love affair with embellished, stylized gloves. We’ve now seen the opera glove in all of its forms. But what about gloves bedecked in crystal cuffs, neon hues, faux fur effects, and beyond? They’ve already arrived, on the runways and at retailers.

Consider Givenchy’s leather wonders, which come in ballooning silhouettes that envelop the hands and wrists. Or Prada’s spring/summer 2026 silky satin opera gloves in shades of pale yellow or olive green. With delicate ruching throughout, the formal opera glove gets an ironic, modern, slouched, and louche makeover. Elsewhere, Khaite has shown the power of an opera-length glove in python. Clearly, texture is an embellishment all its own in these kinds of extreme examples.

In some cases, the gloves become the jewelry. In others, jewelry further accents the statement glove, like for Mugler’s fall/winter 2026 show in which models wore kelly green and royal purple scrunched leather gloves up to their elbows, sometimes styled with a big chunky bracelet over top. Elsewhere, some gloves were mismatched: vivid yellow on one hand and brilliant blue on the other. The brand called it a new take on power dressing, which melded codes of the 1980s, 1940s, and 17th-century aristocratic court coats. “Ultimately, this show intends to acknowledge that self-empowerment is the most potent antidote of clarity to the oppressive forces and grey political climate that surrounds us at this very moment,” Mugler creative director Castro Freitas was quoted in the show notes, “and most of all, it’s a manifesto of the highest power that we should never forget to reclaim: FREEDOM.”

Most of all, the statement glove dresses up any neutral wool coat or adds an extra layer of over-the-top theatrics to a statement coat. When in doubt, you could also simply opt for a colorful glove, which the runways have loved in droves lately. Take, for example, Valentino, which has been pumping out short and statement-making silky gloves in shades of ’80s neon pink or dreamy cobalt blue.

Sometimes, all you need is a change of silhouette, too. At Chanel, the fingerless glove has long been a staple, and it’s now become a favorite of superfan clients during street style moments between shows. Elsewhere, the fingerless glove has forever been a celeb favorite, especially when it comes to style legends like Madonna.

“We’ve got every color under the rainbow of gloves and/or a sheer opera-length glove that is in either a matching color or an opposite color of whatever the look is,” Madonna’s stylist Rita Melssen told ELLE in August 2026.

One thing is clear: Statement gloves are the new power accessory, equal parts practical, theatrical, and impossible to ignore.

#6881. Inside a Denver Tudor That Blends English Eccentricity With Family-Friendly Design

Refurbished original details give the renovation a touch of old-soul charm.

When designer Kaitlyn Hoffman and her family returned to the United States after eight years in London, they had grown accustomed to the tighter quarters that city living often dictates. Needless to say, settling into a five-bedroom Tudor in Denver’s Cheesman Park neighborhood was a big leap. Built in 1900, the house was in good condition when Hoffman and her husband bought it, but earlier renovations had left it feeling at odds with its architectural style.

“The biggest design challenge was anticipating how we would actually live in the home,” Hoffman says. Because they renovated the space before moving in, she had to guess at everything from where they’d gather to how the house would work for their two young kids. “Ultimately, it was an exercise in designing for a life we hadn’t quite experienced yet and trusting our instincts about how we wanted to live.”

The nine-month project, which also launched her firm, Studio Proper Design, restored the home’s Tudor character. Hoffman and her partners at Alter Design + Build gutted the primary and powder bathrooms, overhauled the kitchen, and made smaller updates almost everywhere else. Along the way, plenty of the home’s charming original details got a second life: A pair of French doors found in the basement now anchors a custom pantry, and diamond-pattern mullions in the primary bath windows make the room look as though it had always been part of the house.

The result is a home that reflects Hoffman’s signature “Brit-ish” style: layered, collected, and not too precious. “I think the magic is in the mix,” she says, crediting her years abroad with inspiring a fondness for eccentricity, coziness, and humor in her design projects. Even the house’s lore fits the mood. Neighbors insist it’s the former home of ‘60s country-rock group Nitty Gritty Dirt Band—though Hoffman admits she’s never been able to confirm it. “Whether it’s true or not, it’s fun to imagine that these walls might have witnessed a little rock-and-roll history.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Kaitlyn Hoffman of Studio Proper DesignDesign Partner: Alter Design + BuildStylist: Natalie WaradyLocation: Denver, ColoradoThe Space: A five-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom Tudor across 6,345 square feet

A place where the whole family gathers and hosts, this living room is packed with personality and beloved furnishings, many of which have changed hands in the family throughout the years. While the decor is decidedly eclectic, the architecture itself is classic and stately. “We wanted this room to have the elegance of a traditional English drawing room, so we introduced egg-and-dart crown molding and a hand-carved Louis XV–style marble fireplace to give the space a greater sense of formality and architectural presence,” Hoffman shares.

According to Hoffman, this area saw a true “everything but the kitchen cabinets” renovation. Existing wall ovens gave way to a freestanding range with a custom hood, and a new pantry took their place. It’s built around a pair of original French doors she found in the basement, adding key storage while hiding away the appliances.

To soften the modern lines, she added a paneled backsplash, unlacquered brass hardware, and sealed walnut butcher block countertops for a warm, rustic touch. “I wanted a kitchen that worked hard for our family but felt every bit as inviting as the rest of the house,” Hoffman adds.

Though the dining room shares space with the living room, both areas needed to have distinct identities. To make guests feel like they’re entering into a completely separate room, Hoffman sourced several statement-making pieces. “I found all six antique Louis-style dining chairs on Facebook Marketplace for just $100, while the mirrored faux-bamboo armoire is a favorite piece we brought back with us from London,” Hoffman says.

Tucked beneath the stairs between the kitchen and living room is a clever nook for mixing up the drink of the hour. “This multipurpose space houses my husband’s (many!) coffee supplies by day and transforms into a bar by night,” Hoffman says. Like in the kitchen, she preserved the existing cabinetry and gave it a fresh coat of blue paint, new Negresco honed granite countertops, wallpaper, and updated fixtures. Now, it rivals the kitchen as the go-to entertaining spot in the home.

While gut-renovating this powder room, Hoffman fell in love with the botanical wallpaper, and she would’ve covered the entire space in it if it were a little more kid-friendly. “I opted for wainscoting on the lower half of the walls to protect the wallpaper from sticky fingers,” she says.

This is one of two generously sized guest bedroom suites, so friends and family will never feel short-changed when it’s time to hit the hay. It also has some of the most intriguing decorative details in the entire house. “The gingham four-poster bed is the star of the room, and I love the playful contrast between its bold check and the floral Roman shades,” she says. The wall accents are equally as enviable. “The framed Hermès scarf is another longtime favorite, which I sourced at Round Top over a decade ago, before Round Top was cool!”

Hoffman took a light-touch approach to her daughter’s room, knowing that the seven-year-old would eventually want to put her own stylistic signature on it. “I wanted to create a room that felt charming and playful without being overly childish, with pieces she could continue to enjoy as she grows,” she notes. Beyond painting the walls a pretty blush pink, the only real change here was adding custom Roman shades, which give the room a more tailored, finished feel.

This primary bathroom saw a full gut renovation to bring it up to the standards of modern living. Along with adding his-and-hers sinks and a freestanding tub, Hoffman took the opportunity to correct an architectural eyesore: the two mismatched windows that created irritating asymmetry. “We ultimately replaced both windows, incorporating diamond-pattern mullions in the upper third to complement the home’s Tudor architecture,” she adds. “It was a significant expense, but it gave us greater flexibility with the layout and brought a sense of continuity to the room.”

This guest room is an homage to the family’s life in London, as much of the decor came from their former home across the pond. The art, too, was sourced from a local printmaker whose studio was down the street in the family’s old neighborhood. Any new additions here were smaller and strategic. “The Schumacher floral fabric on the curtains and pillows became the thread that tied everything together, bringing color, pattern, and a sense of cohesion to the room,” Hoffman says.

The kids’ bathroom was reworked with total flexibility in mind. “With a son and a daughter sharing the space, I wanted a palette that felt playful and fresh without leaning either too feminine or masculine,” Hoffman notes. While the existing floor tile stayed, she added a custom double vanity so both kids can brush their teeth at the same time, as well as painted beadboard wainscoting to extend the country-home atmosphere into the washroom.

Kaitlyn Hoffman is the founder of Studio Proper, a boutique residential interior design firm located in Denver, Colorado. She’s a British citizen and lifelong Anglophile whose signature style was honed over nearly a decade living in London. Her design sensibility takes inspiration from traditional English interiors but adds a modern, practical twist.
