#6880. When Does Spotify Wrapped Come Out? Find Out the 2026 Release Date and How to Make Sure You Don’t Miss Yours

It’s nearly Thanksgiving, so Spotify users are expecting their Spotify Wrapped any day now. The campaign—which includes a summary of your listening habits for the year—usually drops around the end of November or the beginning of December, so it can’t be long before it’s released.

So, when will Spotify Wrapped come out this year? How can you make sure that you don't miss yours? And when is the data collected?

Read on to find out everything you need to know about Spotify Wrapped.

The streaming service has yet to confirm an exact date for this year’s Spotify Wrapped, but based on previous years, we can make some educated guesses on when it might be.

Over the years, Wrapped has been released in late November or early December, so we expect it to follow the same pattern for 2026.

Below are all the release dates since Spotify Wrapped first dropped in 2016:

A majority of these dates fell on a Wednesday, so December 2 is looking very promising for 2026.

Spotify Wrapped is an annual campaign provided by the streaming site, giving readers a summary of their listening habits for the past year.

It usually includes five musicians, songs, and musical genres that the user has listened to over the year in a fun graphic that you can then share on social media.

Spotify Wrapped first dropped in 2016, as a rebrand of 2015’s “Year in Music.”

Speaking ahead of the 2024 drop, Spotify’s vice president of partnerships and marketing Marc Hazan said, “What started as a look back at the music and audio that defined the year has become a global cultural phenomenon in its own right, a moment driven by the passion of hundreds of millions of fans. We’re thrilled to debut 2024 Spotify Wrapped to more fans than ever before this year, celebrating the hundreds of millions of ways that they’ve streamed.”

You’ll want to make sure that you have the most updated version of the app so that you can get your Spotify Wrapped for 2026.

To ensure that you're using the newest version of the app, go to the app store on your phone and check for available updates.

With artists like Bad Bunny, Drake, and Taylor Swift predicted to top the 2026 lists due to major album releases and tours, you won’t want to miss your roundup.

Spotify has never confirmed the exact start and end dates for its listening data window for Spotify Wrapped. However, we do know that they start collecting data at the start of January and conclude sometime in November.

What is known is that Spotify begins collecting data at the start of January and concludes sometime in November.

Speaking to Mashable during a Wrapped press presentation in December 2024, the senior director of product Molly Holder explained, “We start at the beginning of January with all of your listening that begins right then at the beginning of the year, and it goes all the way through collecting data up until a few weeks before we launch the Wrapped product.”

This means Spotify likely stops collecting data for its annual Wrapped campaign in early to mid-November, so the music you listen to after Wrapped and before January 1 may not count.

#6877. Which Ultra-Processed Foods Are the Worst for You? A Dietitian Breaks It Down

From hot dogs to whole-grain breads, here are the ultra-processed foods worth limiting first.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Research over the years has shown exactly how ultra-processed foods (UPFs) negatively affect the body. What’s still being studied is how many of these specific foods, and how much of them, impact the rise in certain cancers and other diseases. Although the internet is rife with stats showing an uptick in certain cancers among younger adults, a recent study conducted among a massive 460,000 Americans debunked one of the many fear-mongering rumors that ultra-processed foods are associated with increased colorectal cancer risk specifically. But before you place your next fast food delivery order, Caitlin Hosmer, MS, RD, LDN, a registered dietitian and co-founder of MenoPantry alongside Mark Gilbert, nutritionist from The 1:1 Diet by Cambridge Weight Plan, explains which parts of the meal are potentially the most harmful, and what you should know about the study’s findings to understand which ultra-processed foods are actually the worst for you.

Most researchers use a system called NOVA, developed by a Brazilian epidemiologist, Carlos Augusto Monteiro, who coined the term “ultra-processed foods.” Hosmer explains that NOVA sorts foods into four groups based on how much they’ve been processed and not by their nutrients.

Gilbert argues that the system is questionable. “Even nutrition experts were unable to categorize foods reliably or consistently into one of [these] four categories, from unprocessed to ultra-processed. Other systems, including IFIC, Nutri-Score, and the proposed CHIPS system, exist but are similarly controversial,” says Gilbert.

To better understand what these foods are exactly, Hosmer says the simplest way to put it is that a processed food is still recognizably food that’s been preserved or prepared. An ultra-processed food has been taken apart. “These foods are designed to be cheap, shelf-stable, convenient, and very easy to overeat. The key caveat is that NOVA describes how a food is made, not how nutritious it is. So the Ultra-processed category can hold both a hot dog and a packaged whole-grain bread,” says Hosmer.

Gilbert further explains that the processes many of these foods undergo often reduce healthy, essential nutrients, remove fiber, and make the resulting food easier to consume, absorb, and digest, leading to untoward blood sugar levels, less satiety and, in the longer term, poorer metabolic health.

Gilbert notes that it’s difficult to deem any one type of UPF as “worst” simply because genetic and environmental risk factors to various diseases and ill health vary so vastly. “So, just like no single diet is ‘best’ for everyone, no single food is necessarily worst. For instance, if someone has a high heart and vascular disease risk, they might benefit from less saturated fat. If someone has a high risk of diabetes, they should avoid sugar and other refined carbohydrates that raise their blood sugar levels,” Gilbert explains.

However, Hosmer points out that when researchers break ultra-processed foods into subgroups, processed meat consistently comes out on top. The aforementioned study of more than 460,000 adults, conducted by the National Cancer Institute (NCI), found that ultra-processed food intake overall wasn’t linked to colorectal cancer—but higher consumption of meat from ultra-processed food sources was. Hosmer references a 2024 Harvard analysis in The British Medical Journal that found the same pattern for mortality.

“Across more than 30 years of follow-up, meat-, poultry-, and seafood-based ready-to-eat products showed the strongest and most consistent associations, followed by sugar-sweetened and artificially sweetened beverages, dairy-based desserts, and ultra-processed breakfast food. That category includes processed meats such as hot dogs, deli meats, bacon, sausages, and many frozen breaded meat products,” Hosmer summarizes.

Second to processed meat are sweetened drinks—both sugar-sweetened and artificially sweetened. “Sodas, sweet teas, energy drinks, and diet drinks give you sweetness and calories (or the expectation of calories) with no fiber, protein, or real food structure. They show up consistently across heart, metabolic, and mortality research,” Hosmer notes.

Sugar-laced and dairy-based desserts alongside sugary breakfast foods follow on the list. “Many sugary cereals and pastries are basically dessert dressed up as breakfast,” says Hosmer.

These foods won’t necessarily give you cancer or lead to other diseases; Hosmer explains that the more useful message is that “worst” means low in what the body needs and high in what it doesn’t. In the NCI study, higher intake of whole grains, fiber, dairy products, and calcium from ultra-processed food sources was linked to a lower incidence of colorectal cancer. “A packaged whole-grain bread and a package of bologna are both “ultra-processed,” but they are not the same choice,” she adds.

Many UPFs are often made to be irresistible, meaning you’ll likely eat more than the recommended serving size. Gilbert explains that this leads to large spikes in blood sugar and doesn’t reduce satiety as much as most whole foods do. UPFs can also increase overall calorie intake. “Increased calorie intake and excess blood sugar spikes cause weight gain, high blood pressure, inflammation, diabetes, unhealthy blood lipids, generally poor metabolic health, and a variety of other effects which increase a person’s odds of the most common serious health conditions and diseases, reducing health span and lifespan,” Gilbert warns.

To illustrate, Hosmer points to what she calls a small but elegant 2019 National Institutes of Health (NIH) trial led by Kevin Hall, which she says has the strongest causal evidence. Twenty adults lived on a metabolic ward and ate either an ultra-processed or a minimally processed diet for two weeks each. The diets were matched in calories, sugar, fat, fiber, and sodium, and people could eat as much as they wanted. On the ultra-processed diet, participants ate about 500 more calories a day, ate faster, and gained weight—about two pounds; on the minimally processed diet, they lost about the same amount. “That points to something about the food’s form, not just its nutrients: it’s soft, energy-dense, quick to eat, and slow to trigger fullness,” says Hosmer.

Considering these results, she says there are multiple mechanisms present that likely work together:

Turn the package over and read the ingredient list. That tells you far more than the front of the box. Some signs:

Context matters too. A whole-grain bread with one dough conditioner is a very different food from a toaster pastry, even though both may technically count as ultra-processed. Ask two questions: how processed is it, and what is it actually giving me? Fiber, protein, whole grains, and added nutrients count for a lot.

This depends greatly on the specific foods in question. “That’s what makes this new study that differentiates between the different kinds of processed foods particularly important,” says Hosmer. “The clearest evidence is for processed meat. In 2015, the World Health Organization’s International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC) classified processed meat as a Group 1 carcinogen for colorectal cancer. That’s the category for strong evidence that something causes cancer, though it says nothing about how big the risk is,” Hosmer clarifies. More practically, she further explains that the International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC) estimated that eating 50 grams of processed meat every day—about one hot dog or a few slices of deli meat regularly—is associated with roughly an 18 percent increase in baseline colorectal cancer risk. “That’s a relative increase: for an average person, it moves a lifetime risk from approximately four to five percent to roughly five to six percent,” says Hosmer.

For ultra-processed foods as a whole category, Hosmer says the picture is less clear. The new NCI study is one of the largest ever done on this question. When the team looked by food group, whole grains and dairy were linked to lower risk and meat to higher risk. The researchers note possible opposing effects of some ultra-processed foods on colorectal cancer etiology—mainly that the helpful and harmful foods cancel each other out in the total. More specifically, the total consumption may matter more than the specific foods or food groups.

According to Hosmer, there’s also an indirect pathway. “Diets high in ultra-processed foods are linked to obesity, and excess body fat is an established risk factor for many cancers, including breast cancer after menopause, endometrial cancer, and colorectal cancer,” she notes. Hosmer warns, though, that this study’s findings shouldn’t be read as ultra-processed foods are fine. “Read it as a reminder that it’s hard to make a single recommendation about such a large and varied category of food.” When the researchers broke ultra-processed foods down by type, the results lined up with what we already know: whole grains, fiber, and dairy were linked to lower colorectal cancer risk, and processed meat to higher risk. Hosmer’s challenge is to check the new data against well-established research. “Headlines alone can be misleading, especially on topics as emotionally charged as what we eat.”

Turn the package over and read the ingredient list. That tells you far more than the front of the box. Here’s what to look for:

Context matters too. A whole-grain bread with one dough conditioner is a very different food from a toaster pastry, even though both may technically count as ultra-processed. Hosmer says to ask yourself two questions: “How processed is it, and what is it actually giving me?” Fiber, protein, whole grains, and added nutrients count for a lot.

Hosmer explains that the risks associated with ultra-processed foods include two things: how much of someone’s diet comes from these foods, and how much room their body has to absorb the negative effects.

One caveat from the new study that Hosmer points out is that participants were mostly older white Americans, so results may not generalize to younger or more diverse populations. “That matters because colorectal cancer is rising in adults under 50, the group that has grown up recently, and with the most ultra-processed food available,” she cautions.

Gilbert adds that knowing your family’s health history and getting various tests can offer some indications of individual risks, but predicting these risks is by no means foolproof. “Ultimately, those who have prediabetes, diabetes, high blood pressure, cardiovascular disease or cancer, or are living with overweight or obesity are the most at risk because these conditions can often be avoided or improved with better nutrition,” says Gilbert.

Both Gilbert and Hosmer agree here. “Some foods can be intentionally processed to increase and improve their nutritional profile rather than reduce it,” Gilbert explains. Frozen vegetables, canned beans, plain yogurt, canned salmon, and bagged salad are strong examples. They are processed and still very good for you. Processing often makes healthy food more affordable and accessible. Gilbert’s 1:1 Diet is designed to align with this. “The products we produce are processed, but instead of reducing the essential nutrient content of the products, we actually increase it, adding vitamins, minerals, essential fats, and quality protein,” Gilbert notes, adding that extensive research has shown his diet improves blood sugar, blood pressure, inflammation, metabolic health, cardiovascular health, and reverses diabetes in about half of dieters.

Myth: This new study proves ultra-processed food is safe.

Hosmer says explicitly that it doesn’t. “It looked at only one disease, colorectal cancer, in a mostly older population, and participants reported their diets just once, back in the mid-1990s. The authors themselves point out that ultra-processed foods became far more common and varied over the following two decades, so the study likely underestimated how much people were actually eating,” she clarifies. “The fairer reading is that which ultra-processed foods you eat matters more than the category itself: a whole-grain bread and a hot dog may both be “ultra-processed,” but they don’t affect the body the same way.”

Myth: It’s only about nutrients, and processing doesn’t matter.

Kevin Hall’s controlled feeding trial suggests otherwise. People ate significantly more on the ultra-processed diet even when nutrients were matched, so a food’s structure and how fast you eat it matter too.

Myth: Organic, plant-based, or “clean label” means it isn’t ultra-processed.

Myth: You have to completely eliminate ultra-processed foods.

Perfection isn’t the goal. Hosmer points out that in the NCI study, replacing 10 percent of the total weight of ultra-processed foods with minimally processed foods was associated with a 2 percent lower incidence of colorectal cancer. “We don’t yet know exactly what that looks like on an individual’s plate. The study measured diets statistically, not swap by swap. But the direction is clear, and it fits decades of nutrition research: incremental shifts away from ultra-processed foods appear to matter, even when they’re modest. Start with categories that matter most: processed meats and sweetened drinks,” she concludes.

#6876. 30 Breakup Texts to Send Instead of Ghosting Someone

Figuring out how to tell someone you're not interested shouldn't be harder than the actual breakup.

You went on a date. They were perfectly nice, you had a decent time, and you even said you'd do it again. Except now they're texting you about Friday, and you're realizing you'd actually rather spend the evening doing literally anything else. So you consider taking the path of least resistance: simply never responding again. No awkward conversation, no hurt feelings (at least on your end), and no need to figure out how to tell someone you're just not that into them.

Unfortunately, there's a reason sending a breakup text feels harder than disappearing. “We ghost because we would rather avoid the interaction completely,” explains Kayden Kross, founder of Gasplighting and author of Deeper. And while ghosting might save you an uncomfortable interaction, it leaves the other person wondering whether you're busy, uninterested, or just never planning to text them back.

To be clear, we're talking about casual dating and early-stage connections here, not ending a years-long relationship with a two-sentence text. And if someone makes you feel unsafe or repeatedly disrespects your boundaries, you don't owe them an explanation.

But if the only thing stopping you from ending things is that you don't know what to say, we've got you. From the person who won't stop asking for a second date to the one who only texts after midnight, here are 30 breakup texts to send instead of ghosting.

Short answer: Yes, but context matters. If you've only been on a date or two, there's no need to schedule an in-person breakup conversation with someone you're still getting to know. “If the majority of the interaction has been by text or DM then ending it by text is within bounds,” says Kross.

But if you've been seeing someone for months, have developed real feelings, or are in an established relationship, a phone call or face-to-face conversation is generally more considerate. The more time and emotional investment involved, the more care the breakup deserves.

Of course, there's one exception to any breakup etiquette rule: your safety. If someone has made you feel unsafe, threatened you, or repeatedly ignored your boundaries, you don't need to meet up, get on a call, or even respond just to be polite.

The best breakup texts are short, honest, and leave very little room for interpretation. You don't need to write a five-paragraph essay about why you're not feeling it, but you do need to make it clear that you're ending things. “It’s more about being clear that we’re not interested in continuing so the other side isn’t left in a position of uncertainty about what’s going on,” Kross explains.

One easy approach? Acknowledge the time you've spent together, briefly explain that you don't see things moving forward, and wish them well. No need to list everything you didn't like about them or invent an excuse to soften the blow.

And whatever you do, don't leave the door open if you know it's closed. “To me, the most painful thing in relationships is false hope,” says Kross. Skip the “maybe someday” and “let's stay friends” unless you genuinely mean them. Being clear might feel uncomfortable now, but it's a lot kinder than keeping someone guessing.

Seriously. If someone has made you feel unsafe, harassed you, or continued contacting you after you've asked them to stop, you don't owe them another explanation. Protecting your peace and safety is more important than following breakup etiquette.

#6875. How to Watch Every Movie in the Marvel Cinematic Universe

It’s easy. You only need time. And a Disney+ subscription.

Suddenly, we’re excited about Marvel again. Hitting theaters on December 18 is Avengers: Doomsday, the long-awaited next Avengers sequel. You may have heard that Robert Downey Jr. is back, only now in the role of the villainous Doctor Doom instead of Iron Man. As the man himself said back at San Diego Comic-Con in July 2024: “New mask, same task.” Whatever that means. (Also, if you think you ever heard of something called Avengers: The Kang Dynasty, no, you didn’t.)

Anyway, it’s been seven years since we last saw a new Avengers movie. Back then, you might have Googled something like, “How to watch every Marvel movie.” I have a gut feeling that’s what brought you here now. As Doomsday awakens dormant feelings about superheroes and multiverses, you probably need a guide to help you revisit all the best Marvel films or catch up on the ones you’ve missed. Lucky for you, it is easy to catch up. You just need time and a Disney+ subscription. If you start now, you’ll roll credits on The Fantastic Four: First Steps by December 17.

Here’s how to watch every Marvel movie before Avengers: Doomsday. Check back a year from now, when we’re doing it all over again for Avengers: Secret Wars.

Here’s the good news: Except for Spider-Man: Brand New Day, which came out this summer and is now on digital, every Marvel Cinematic Universe movie is available on Disney+. Every. Single. One.

Iron Man? Yes. Infinity War? Yup. Captain America: The Winter Soldier? Hell yeah. Guardians of the Galaxy? All three, baby! Ant-Man and the Wasp: Quantumania? Maybe skip that one. And since Disney acquired Fox in 2018, you’ll also find all the X-Men, Deadpool, and the two Fantastic Four movies from the mid-2000s.

There’s also plenty of Marvel television. Not just streaming shows like WandaVision, The Falcon and the Winter Soldier, Ms. Marvel, and the recent VisionQuest, but also network shows like Agents of S.H.I.E.L.D. and Agent Carter, as well as the Netflix Defenders-verse. If you’re really hardcore, you can also watch the handful of “One Shot” short films that were originally released on several Blu-rays.

The only “Marvel movies” you can’t watch on Disney+ are (most) of the Sony-produced movies. These include Morbius, Madame Web, Kraven the Hunter, and the Venom trilogy. But because distribution rights are funny, the Tobey Maguire and Andrew Garfield Spider-Man movies are on Disney+, along with Spider-Man: Across the Spider-Verse as I write this.

I hear you. Short of time-traveling back to 2000 and watching every movie as they come out, it feels impossible to “catch up” with all of it now. Especially since Avengers: Doomsday is once again dealing with the multiverse, which means even “non-canon” movies (like, say, the Blade or Punisher movies) might get a shout-out.

Luckily, Marvel knows your pain. On Disney+, a new “Countdown to Avengers: Doomsday” sub-page highlights the most important movies. While there are glaring omissions, watching only the highlighted movies can bump just about anyone up to speed. Even people who say they’ve “never seen one of these things.”

For your convenience, I’ve listed below EVERY movie from the Fox X-Men universe, the Sony Spider-Man universes, and the Marvel Cinematic Universe, in order of theatrical release. This is the only correct “viewing order.” Do not listen to YouTubers who insist on some “timeline order” nonsense. You can be an adult and watch these things exactly how they were produced and released. The movies Marvel deems essential for Doomsday prep are listed in bold, with the trailers attached.

Blade is rightfully credited with kickstarting the 21st-century superhero boom, but the X-Men films set the tone as must-see summer blockbusters. The first film in Fox’s X-Men series introduced audiences to mutants, with Hugh Jackman as Wolverine, the late Patrick Stewart as Charles Xavier, and Ian McKellen as radical extremist Magneto, who leads his Brotherhood in a fight for equal rights. Famke Janssen and James Marsden, who also starred as Jean Grey and Cyclops, respectively, will return in Doomsday, but Halle Berry’s Storm is sitting it out.

The battle for mutant liberation continues when Colonel Stryker (Brian Cox) kidnaps Xavier, forcing the X-Men to align with the Brotherhood of Mutants. In this better-than-you-remember sequel, mutantkind stands side by side for the sake of their survival. Though X2 predates shared universes, its scope, scale, and political intrigue make it one of the greatest and most underrated sequels of all time.

“I am Iron Man.” The resurrection of Robert Downey Jr.’s career blasted off to the stratosphere, along with the Marvel Cinematic Universe itself, in a movie that officially kicked off the MCU. Kidnapped by terrorists and forced into a cave with scraps, playboy futurist Tony Stark (Downey Jr.) escapes as the armored superhero Iron Man and returns home to find that his enemies are far closer to him than he suspects. When the smoke clears, a shadowy figure named Nick Fury (Samuel L. Jackson) informs him that the universe is way bigger than he knows.

Once, Chris Evans was the Human Torch. Suddenly, he wasn’t. As the Marvel Cinematic Universe picked up steam, the sweltering summer of 2011 saw Captain America: The First Avenger. Set in World War II, the first Marvel “prequel” stars Evans as the brave shrimp of Brooklyn, Steve Rogers, who becomes the first to endure the experimental “Super Soldier” program. As Captain America, Rogers takes the fight to Red Skull, whose dark ambition lies beyond the Third Reich.

There once was an idea: To bring together a remarkable group of cinematic superheroes to see if they can make cinematic history. In 2012, Marvel did just that. The first crossover Avengers movie saw the growing roster of Marvel’s cinematic superheroes team up for the very first time. Though Marvel movies have only gotten bigger and even better since, we cannot deny the seismic impact of The Avengers.

Captain America: The Winter Soldier (2014)

After The Avengers planted the seeds for Thanos, all of Marvel’s storytelling shifted to an adaptation of the 1991 comic crossover The Infinity Gauntlet. In 2018, Marvel released Avengers: Infinity War, the third Avengers movie that forever changed the MCU’s status quo. With Josh Brolin as Thanos, the Marvel Universe’s many heroes band together to stave off the greatest threat anyone had ever seen. While audiences knew another movie was around the corner, its shock ending dared to ask the unthinkable: What happens when heroes lose?

It all came down to this. In 2019, on the same weekend Game of Thrones staged its own final battle, Avengers: Endgame changed the trajectory of the Marvel Universe once more. The fourth Avengers film sees the heroes dust off for a rematch against Thanos. But before they can do that, they must travel back in time through their own history to collect the Infinity Stones if they want to stand a chance.

In September 2026, in preparation for Doomsday, Marvel released an extended cut of Avengers: Endgame, titled Encore. It comes with a few extra minutes of scenes that frankly ruin the grace of Endgame in its original form, but if you really want to see clearer connective tissue to Doomsday, check out Encore.

In the 1970s, one of the most popular Marvel Comics titles wasn’t The Amazing Spider-Man, Uncanny X-Men, or The Avengers. It was Master of Kung Fu, starring an ass-kicker named Shang-Chi. He was a master martial artist and the son of a supervillain (Fu Manchu in the comics) who, over time, works as a secret agent for MI6. He was created by Steve Englehart and Jim Starlin (who also created Thanos) in the spirit of Bruce Lee and the TV show Kung Fu.

After decades of obscurity, the box-office success of Crazy Rich Asians in 2018 inspired Marvel to dig into the longboxes and put the spotlight back on Shang-Chi. In 2021, Simu Liu brought him to life in Shang-Chi and the Legend of the Ten Rings, an origin movie that introduces Shang-Chi for the 21st century. Hong Kong screen legend Tony Leung (In the Mood for Love) costars as Wenwu, Shang-Chi’s father, who runs the terrorist group the Ten Rings—named after Wenwu’s mysterious alien weapons—which kidnapped Tony Stark in the first Iron Man.

Shang-Chi’s use of the Ten Rings has yet-unknown ties to the wider Marvel Universe. After five years, we may finally have some answers in Avengers: Doomsday.

After Spider-Man was publicly unmasked as Peter Parker at the end of Spider-Man: Far From Home, the teenage superhero turns to Sorcerer Supreme Doctor Strange (Benedict Cumberbatch) for help. But when the magic goes wrong, the multiverse cracks open, and villains from other realities—as in, other Spider-Man franchises—seep through the cracks. Tom Holland’s Spider-Man teams up with two veteran Spideys, played by Tobey Maguire and Andrew Garfield (remember them?), to put the universe back in one piece.

Doctor Strange saved the universe in Avengers: Endgame and restored the multiverse in Spider-Man: No Way Home. But can he save himself? In this direct sequel to 2016’s Doctor Strange and set after both Spider-Man: No Way Home and the Disney+ series WandaVision, Stephen must help protect a young girl, America Chavez (Xochitl Gomez), from a vengeful Wanda (Elizabeth Olsen), who wants to use America’s unique powers to reunite with her own lost family.

Originally billed as Marvel’s first horror movie (its very title alluding to Lovecraft’s At the Mountains of Madness), creative reshuffling led to Evil Dead and Spider-Man director Sam Raimi helming the movie. This explains the movie’s uniquely darker tone than one might expect from a typical Marvel tentpole. Cameos in this film tie directly to some of Marvel’s other far corners, like TV shows What If...? and Inhumans, as well as the Fox X-Men movies.

Werewolf by Night (2022), a “Marvel Studios Special Presentation” on Disney+

The real-life passing of Chadwick Boseman in 2020 left a huge hole in everyone’s hearts—and Marvel in a difficult place. Without its star, writer/director Ryan Coogler redesigned his plans for the sequel to his Earth-shattering 2018 hit.

Taking inspiration from Reginald Hudlin’s run in the comics, Black Panther: Wakanda Forever sets the stage for a new Black Panther: Shuri, T’Challa’s genius sister, played by Letitia Wright. But before Shuri can take up her brother’s mantle, she must first learn how heavy crowns are when another kingdom emerges from the depths and threatens Wakanda. The sequel introduces Namor (Tenoch Huerta), from the underwater world Talokan (originally Atlantis in the comics). The two will play a larger role in Avengers: Doomsday.

The Guardians of the Galaxy Holiday Special (2022), a “Marvel Studios Special Presentation” on Disney+

After Hugh Jackman retired from playing Wolverine in the 2017 masterpiece Logan, Marvel and Ryan Reynolds shelled out a truckload of money to get him back and into some yellow spandex at last. The third movie in the Deadpool series, Deadpool & Wolverine, marked the MCU debut of Ryan Reynolds’ scrotum-faced Deadpool, whose dream of joining the Avengers clashes with the collapse of his own reality. More cameos, callbacks, and cusses fill the movie’s two-hour runtime.

While Jackman and Channing Tatum—who was once tapped to play the X-Men member Gambit, which Deadpool & Wolverine finally made a reality—will return in Doomsday, it’s unknown whether Reynolds will too. But if his surprise appearance at Comic-Con means anything, then...

When Steve Rogers gave Sam Wilson (Anthony Mackie) the shield of Captain America in Endgame, Sam wasn’t ready to take up the responsibility so soon. He still had his own things to work out. After the Disney+ series The Falcon and the Winter Soldier, Sam now flies the skies as Captain America, with the 2025 film Captain America: Brave New World marking the start of his era as the Star-Spangled Man. In addition to being the fourth Captain America movie, the movie revisits the events of 2008’s The Incredible Hulk and 2021’s Eternals.

The Marvel franchise was built on a studio elevating comic book benchwarmers into A-listers. That tradition was upheld in the 2025 movie Thunderbolts*. Based on a Marvel Comics series where supervillains form a team, the big-screen version recruits several “forgotten” antiheroes from different corners of the MCU. Together, they form a new team that must stave off their own assassination and save the world—on their terms. You can watch the movie cold, but know that many of these characters come from movies like Captain America: The Winter Soldier, Black Widow, Ant-Man and the Wasp, and the TV show The Falcon and the Winter Soldier.

And now, we step into a whole ‘nother world. After multiple attempts at a big-screen Fantastic Four, the Marvel Cinematic Universe launched its own take on the First Family in The Fantastic Four: First Steps. Set on a different Earth in 1964, the Fantastic Four rise to the occasion when the planet is threatened by Galactus.

Look. Don’t put yourself through that. There are too many. If you insist on watching any of them, just watch Loki. It has two seasons and just 12 episodes total, so you can knock it out in one weekend. Loki takes place after Avengers: Endgame (technically after just The Avengers, but how and why is too long to explain), and of all the shows, it bears the strongest ties to Doomsday. Talk about a glorious purpose.

#6872. An Up-Close Look at The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power’s Twisted Love Triangle

Morfydd Clark, Jamie Campbell Bower, and Charlie Vickers unpack that tree nest scene.

This story contains spoilers for The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power.

After sleeping alone, it takes some getting used to sharing a bed with someone. This is a challenging proposition in the third season of The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power for the recently reunited Galadriel (Morfydd Clark) and her husband, Celeborn (Jamie Campbell Bower), when they use a tree nest as a makeshift bedchamber, as the Dark Lord Sauron (Charlie Vickers) hunts them down. But Elves are not mere mortals, and after centuries apart, they slip back into slumbering beside each other as if not a day has passed. On set, however, things were much more awkward: Galadriel’s sword gets in Clark’s way, she struggles to roll over in chainmail, and the couple’s moss pillow is too small.

It is mid-September 2025 at the legendary Shepperton Studios just outside London, and there has been another request for more real moss to elevate Bower’s head. (He likens this grassy pillow to the mattresses in The Princess and the Pea when we talk a year later.) The mood up in the tree nest, as Clark and Bower rehearse a scene from the fifth episode, is much lighter than what plays out on screen. Under the watchful eyes of director Sanaa Hamri, the actors figure out the logistics of spooning in this confined space, mimicking sleep so the camera can see their faces, and allowing Clark to shift to her side, then quickly back away when Galadriel imagines Sauron lying beside her. Later in the day, Vickers will switch in. The shock sends Galadriel falling backward out of the tree and straight into danger. This is not your run-of-the-mill love triangle or burgeoning throuple.

To put it mildly, Galadriel has been going through it in the adaptation of J.R.R. Tolkien’s history of Middle-earth. After she rejected Sauron’s offer to be his queen (again) at the end of season 2, he has since taken much of Middle-earth, and his quest to create the One Ring—and thereby rule over all—continues. In the previous episode, he attempted to locate an elf being held prisoner in Gundabad who knew the secrets of forging the One Ring. It turns out the last Elven-smith was already dead, but Galadriel’s long-lost husband, Celeborn, was locked up in a neighboring cell. Celeborn likely knows those same secrets that Sauron needs, making him invaluable. During their escape, Arondir (Ismael Cruz Córdova) is slain, and Sauron comes face-to-face with Galadriel for the first time this season. Well, other than popping into her subconscious.

When Sauron appears in Galadriel’s dreams again in the fifth episode, “The Lady of Lórien,” the safety of the tree canopy is immediately shattered as Galadriel’s sleeping mind pictures Sauron lying inches away where she expects to see Celeborn. When I catch up with Clark on Zoom a year later in September 2026, her face lights up when I mention this scene. “I loved being up there [in the tree nest],” she says. “It was so cozy.” While the episode ends with Celeborn being captured by Sauron—separating the couple once again—there is a brief respite from the horrors of this war-torn land. “There’s a real unabashed lean into the gorgeousness of fantasy,” Clark says of this season, including the foray into swoon-worthy romance in the cocooned foliage.

While the Elves have only just reunited, Clark and Bower had already established the foundation of Galadriel and Celeborn’s relationship during a dance sequence in the season 3 premiere episode, revealing a far lighter Galadriel than we have seen for some time. This also gave the actors an opportunity to get to know each other. “There is a magic about him,” Clark says of Bower. “We did get to rehearse that together, which also took away a certain type of magic in that learning a dance with someone is pretty vulnerable. We got to know each other doing that, which was really nice. He was so lovely, and he is elf-like.”

As we learned in season 1, Celeborn left to fight in the war during the First Age and never came home—Middle-earth is now in its Second Age. Galadriel and Celeborn share battle stories, but it is Sauron informing Celeborn that they have previously met (Celeborn claims he has no memory of this) that throws more fire onto this already sizzling dynamic. But before Sauron storms in like a jealous ex, Celeborn and Galadriel manage some K-I-S-S-I-N-G in the tree. “It was a tight space,” Bower laughs over the phone earlier this month. “Towards the end of that scene, there’s that beautiful moment of physical reconnection, and our intimacy coordinator [Sophie Cooch] was so helpful in finding a beauty and almost like a dance and a grace within that physical reconnection into the falling down [together], and being able to lie side by side,” Bower says.

Having just played Stranger Things’ big bad Vecna, Bower (who is a huge fan of Tolkien’s work) said he was looking for a character who represented love, and Celeborn radiates light even in the darkest circumstances. In the episode, Celeborn senses Galadriel is holding back, but doesn’t press too hard about what. “She definitely feels like she’s lost a sparkle, and he’s a mirror to her,” says Clark. “She can’t ignore the parts of it that have changed.” Clark recalls discussing with Bower that there should be zero “feeling of ownership between them.” When I mention this to Bower, he references the line from Breakfast at Tiffany’s to sum up this dynamic: “We belong to nobody, and nobody belongs to us. We don’t even belong to each other.”

Tolkien’s marriage and experience in World War I provided inspiration for this union, but it was The Magnetic Fields song “Abigail, Belle of Kilronan” that Clark says she became obsessed with while filming. “It’s about someone going off to war, and it’s like, ‘And when I come home, if I come home, you’ll be a grown woman. And when I come home, if I come home, don’t be alone, Abigail, Belle of Kilronan,’” Clark says. “That’s almost been her biggest betrayal of him that she allowed herself to be lonely for so long, which I found really beautiful.”

One person Galadriel was close to before knowing his real identity was Sauron. Although she vocalizes that she doesn’t want Sauron present in this moment—not even uttering his name—the image of him lying next to her is a jolt back to reality, even if he is a figment of her imagination. “Even though in many ways her dreams are coming true with being reunited with Celeborn,” Clark says, “they can’t live in a simple vacuum of happiness. The world is still out there, and she’s part of the world.” Unfortunately for Galadriel, as she quickly backs away, she falls from the tree, alerting the nearby orcs to their presence.

The vision seems to be Galadriel’s own doing; Sauron is living rent-free in her mind instead of intentionally popping into her dreams. Vickers discussed this approach with Clark. “The way I play it was determined by what she [Clark] had in her mind—because it’s Galadriel’s experience, it’s not Sauron’s experience—and that was to be the scariest I could be in Galadriel’s mind,” Vickers says.

When I met Vickers on set, he was in costume, grabbing a juice from craft services before joining Clark up in the tree nest. Having spent a lot of time on a nearby soundstage, where Sauron’s bleak Barad-dûr lair is located, the Australian actor was happy to experience this change of scenery. “Barad-dûr this season is incredibly intense. It was 35 degrees [95°F] on the days we were filming there,” Vickers says over Zoom. “There was a lot of sweat. It was wonderful to go into the forest on a soundstage.” Still, there were some logistical issues for Vickers up in the nest, first finding the same eyeline to match Bower’s sleeping position and then trying not to stab Clark with Sauron’s pointy gloves. “Lying down in all that stuff is incredibly challenging,” Vickers says.

Of course, you don’t see this human behavior in the final cut. “It’s a very particular thing to film something fantasy in this way because the look is so important,” Clark says. “It does matter that their hair looks absolutely lovely in that light, and they move like they’re artists with their bodies.” This includes scenes that might require a stunt double, like the one I observed. “Your stunt double is always a huge part of creating the character, but I think more so than ever with an elf, because she’s got to be excellent,” Clark says. “We do as much as we can with me being as excellent as I can be.” For everything else, Clark’s stunt double Hannah Grant (who has a ballet and martial arts background) steps in.

While the version of Sauron in this scene is a figment of Galadriel’s imagination, the real Dark Lord is traveling to the Eryn Galen woodland. Celeborn sacrifices himself to stop his wife (who is lying unconscious nearby) from being discovered. What comes next will likely be incredibly unpleasant, but Bower enjoyed going head-to-head with Vickers as the twisted trio enters its next phase.

“It’s where sparks can fly,” Bower says. “You need to build a rapport, have a friendship, a comfortability, and a trust in one another. Charlie made that so easy from day one.” However, Sauron won’t be as accommodating. “Celeborn has something that Sauron really needs,” Vickers says. “It’s the key to his whole existence up to this point, so you have all the emotional interplay, but then beneath that you have this real psychological drive, which is, ‘I will do anything. I will not stop. I will do all I can to get this secret out of you.’” It seems unlikely that Celeborn, Galadriel, or Sauron will get any sleep anytime soon.

#6870. Princess Kate Wows in Even More Red on Her Final Day in Mumbai

She’s ending her overseas tour on a high note

Today marks the end of Princess Catherine and Prince William’s three-day trip in Mumbai, India, and for one of her final appearances, the Princess of Wales brought out yet another striking—and meaningful—look.

Early this morning, the royal pair stopped by the Chanakya School of Craft to see a presentation of local, artisan work, before Kate made a solo trip to the Nowrosjee Wadia Maternity Hospital.

For these engagements, the princess looked resplendent in a bright-red wool crepe dress from one of her go-to British brands: Emilia Wickstead.

The design was conceived to look like separates, with a short-sleeve hourglass jacket stitched to a matching A-line skirt with ruffles. The bodice closed with a row of buttons in front, and it featured two decorative pocket flaps and notch lapels along the collar.

This isn’t the first time Kate has worn red on their overseas tour. Just yesterday, she attended the 2026 Earthshot Prize Awards in a vermilion anarkali gown, custom-made by Indian designer Mahima. Traditionally, red has been used to denote purity and power in Indian culture, and it is often a color worn by brides.

To contrast her latest red design, the princess accessorized with chocolate suede slingbacks from Manolo Blahnik, while carrying the custom DeMellier x SurpRice handbag in mocha that she debuted on the first day of their trip.

Finally, she stacked a few golden bangles on her wrist, including a Sézane cuff and a Halcyon Days torque bracelet, which matched her chandelier Cassandra Goad earrings. For a meaningful touch, she also wore a beaded macramé bracelet that was reportedly gifted to her by a local.

For her first overseas tour with Prince William in over four years, Kate has certainly not disappointed with her wardrobe.

#6868. IKEA’s 2027 Color of the Year Is Deeper, Sweeter, and a Little More Daring Than Your Average Neutral

You’ll want to bring this jammy shade right into your kitchen.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As a long year of colorful inspiration winds down, so many design brands are coming out of the woodwork with their 2027 trend predictions. IKEA has just joined the cohort with its 2027 Color of the Year: Wild Fig. Yes, the fiber-rich ficus fruit is setting the standard for the Scandi design destination.

IKEA’s interpretation of the jammy color is deep and dark, with red, lilac, and brown undertones. It’s more imaginative than your typical neutral, but still grounded in nature. “Wild fig weaves together the richness of a deep botanical shade with the personality of a color that isn’t afraid to stand out,” the brand wrote in its release. It fits right in with the earthy color trends we’re anticipating next year.

We’ve been keeping track of all the Color of the Year launches, and there’s a clear theme. Every pick so far can be found in your backyard. Valspar opted for the mid-toned blue of a cloudy sky, while Dutch Boy focused on the reddish-brown clay you’d find in the earth. And there’s been no shortage of greens, from Benjamin Moore’s pine-reminiscent pick to Sherwin-Williams’s bright celery shade. The trends reflect all the peace that’s found in a garden escape, and IKEA’s Wild Fig adds some sweetness and play, daring a bit past the typical tree shades to the fruit that blooms from its leaves.

The key to mastering this color, not unlike the fig jam on your charcuterie board, is what you pair it with. IKEA recommends pairing it with its 2026 Color of the Year, Rebel Pink, or an electric blue for contrast that brings out the character of the shade. And no matter your budget, there are so many easy ways to incorporate the look, from a patterned tray or tablecloth to a low-pile, checked rug. If you want to have some fun with your 2027 refresh, we rounded up some easy pieces to get started below.

#6865. What Is Unrequited Love? Experts Explain Why It Hurts So Much and How to Move On

Because knowing someone doesn't feel the same way doesn't magically make your feelings disappear.

There's nothing quite like having feelings for someone who doesn't feel the same way. Maybe it's a friend you've secretly been into for years, an ex you can't seem to get over, or someone who keeps giving you just enough attention to convince yourself there's still a chance. And while you know, logically, that you can't make someone fall in love with you, that doesn't stop you from overanalyzing every text, replaying every interaction, and wondering whether things might eventually change.

Welcome to the deeply frustrating world of unrequited love. “At its core, unrequited love is a love that’s not reciprocated from one person to another,” explains Leanna Stockard, LMFT, a marriage and family therapist with LifeStance Health. “One person either has stronger feelings for the other or there is no feeling at all from the other.”

And just because your feelings aren't mutual doesn't mean they're any less real or painful. In fact, clinical psychologist Monica Vermani, PsyD, says unrequited love can cause “tremendous emotional turmoil,” leaving you feeling anxious, stressed, or questioning your self-worth.

So, how can you tell if you're experiencing unrequited love versus a particularly intense crush? And how do you move on when part of you is still holding out hope? Here's what experts want you to know about recognizing unrequited love, understanding why it happens, and figuring out what to do next.

Essentially, unrequited love looks like an imbalance of effort, affection, and attention. Stockard says some signs your feelings might not be reciprocated include the other person consistently taking a long time to respond to your texts or calls, avoiding spending time with you, or, in some cases, not even knowing who you are.

Unrequited love often happens between friends, when one person starts developing romantic feelings while the other sees the relationship as strictly platonic. It can also happen when you fall for someone who's unavailable, whether that's someone already in a relationship, an ex, a person who lives far away, an authority figure, or even a celebrity.

If you're wondering whether your feelings are one-sided, here are some signs Vermani says to look out for:

Vermani says unrequited love can sometimes have more to do with our own expectations and unmet emotional needs than the person we're actually falling for.

“Unrequited love is often based on pursuing from a romantic partner what is lacking in our own lives,” she explains. “The reality of this quest for love as a means of filling in the gaps or deficits in our lives (and ourselves) is that it comes from a place of lack. When we set our sights on an ‘ideal’ partner who ticks all the boxes on our must-have list, we often create the ideal conditions for unrequited love.”

Think about it: When you're convinced someone is your perfect match, it's easy to start imagining what your life together could look like, even if the relationship itself doesn't actually exist. You might find yourself focusing on their best qualities, overlooking signs they're not interested, or holding onto the idea that everything would fall into place if they just felt the same way. And sometimes, that imagined relationship can feel easier to hold onto than dealing with the uncertainty and vulnerability of a real one.

“Many of us are brought up to believe that to be happy and fulfilled in life, we need someone—the one—to complete us,” Vermani says. “The main reason we suffer, though, is that we don’t accept people as they are, or accept situations as they are.”

Of course, it's also possible to develop strong feelings for someone without knowing them on a deeper level. You might know their favorite food, birthday, and practically every detail of their Instagram activity, but that doesn't necessarily mean you know what they'd be like as a romantic partner. And even if you're close friends, the intimacy you share isn't necessarily the same as a mutual romantic connection.

The short answer? Your feelings can absolutely be real, even if they're not reciprocated. But there's a difference between loving someone and being in a loving relationship with them. Sometimes, what feels like love can also be a combination of intense longing, attraction, and the idea of what a relationship with that person could look like. And if you don't know them on a deeper level, you might be falling for a version of them that doesn't entirely match reality.

“When love is mutual, there’s balance, give and take, shared intimacy, trust, mutual transparency (meaning a lack of secrecy), and commitment that grows over time,” explains Vermani.

Unrequited love is a pretty common experience, especially when you're still figuring out what traits you value in a potential partner. And having feelings for someone who doesn't feel the same way isn't inherently unhealthy. It becomes more concerning when those feelings start affecting your well-being, preventing you from pursuing other relationships, or making it difficult to respect the other person's boundaries.

“Being in a one-sided relationship may lead to further feelings of rejection and increased anxiety,” Stockard says. And while it's normal to have a hard time letting go, continually holding out hope for a relationship that isn't going to happen can make moving forward even harder. “Unrequited love can be dangerous when a person takes their love to a level such as stalking, manipulation, or other dangerous forms of obsession,” she adds.

That said, not every case of unrequited love turns into something unhealthy. It's entirely possible to care about someone, accept that they don't feel the same way, and eventually move forward without those feelings interfering with your life or theirs.

Just because someone doesn't return your feelings doesn't mean you can magically turn yours off. But if the situation is causing you anxiety or heartbreak, finding ways to refocus your energy can help. After all, continuing to pursue someone who's made it clear they're not interested is only going to make moving on harder.

Stockard says there are a few different approaches you can try, starting with giving yourself some space. “Determine if you need time away from the other person to gain clarity, and respect their needs as well,” she suggests. “As much as your feelings for them are valid, their feelings of not wanting to pursue something further with you are also valid.” That might mean resisting the urge to text them, making plans that don't revolve around them, or limiting contact until your feelings have lessened or gone away.

If you're still struggling, Stockard also recommends seeking therapy. “This process can help you feel confident that a partner who values you and wants you exists, and lessen the fixation on someone who does not,” she explains. Therapy can also help you better understand your relationship patterns and what you're looking for in a partner.

And while it might not feel like it right now, time and perspective can make a difference. “Often unrequited love is a fantasy—an intense, entirely one-sided romantic infatuation—rather than actual love. Over time, infatuations fade and neutralize,” Vermani says. “It’s important to focus on self-love, self-care, and self-fulfillment, because when we’re content with ourselves, we’re able to walk away from unrequited love that’s not in our highest and best interest. Once that happens, we’re able to recognize and find reciprocal love.”

You don't have to stop caring about someone overnight. But you can start making choices that leave room for a relationship where your feelings are actually returned.

#6864. Only Murders in the Building Season 6: Everything We Know So Far

Though Only Murders in the Building wrapped its fifth season in October 2025, the Hulu hit is hardly slowing down. The series is heading toward its next chapter with awards-season momentum after earning Golden Globe nominations for Best Television Series – Musical or Comedy, as well as best performance nods for stars Selena Gomez, Steve Martin, and Martin Short.

The final episode of season 5, which ended with the suspicious death of Cinda Canning, left fans on a major cliffhanger that begs for a deeper investigation. Here’s everything we know so far about the future of the show.

Yes! The news came shortly after the season 5 finale premiered. According to The Hollywood Reporter, the forthcoming season will officially switch locations and be filmed in London. The previous season’s finale ended with the trio listening to a new Cinda Canning podcast set across the pond. That, along with Canning’s mysterious death at the end of the episode, may hint at why the group could travel to the U.K.

Selena Gomez previously stated that she hopes Only Murders continues for many seasons to come. “I really hope that I can just keep evolving Mabel as I get older, because I want to do this for a long time,” she told USA Today in September 2025.

Steve Martin also felt optimistic about a sixth season. “We believe we’re doing another season,” he said. “We think so. But you have to live season by season. And even if it was canceled tomorrow, we would have to say, ‘We had a great, great run. We loved what we were doing. It was a great way to spend five, six years.’”

In May 2026, Gomez shared that filming had officially begun when she reposted a photo from the show’s Instagram account to her Instagram Story. The image, showing Gomez alongside some of the cast members together in London, was captioned, “Season 6 🇬🇧 Day 1!”

Just like previous seasons, season 6 is confirmed to have 10 episodes.

Only Murders in the Building season 6 is taking place in London, as well as a select few countryside locations in England. In an August 2026 interview with Vulture, showrunner Josh Hoffman teased, “one of the more signature episodes is called ‘The Country House,’ and that sits in a very specific vibe and look, and quite a collection of characters.” He added, “We got about an hour outside of London…and into some of the stately homes.”

Season 5 concluded with Cinda Canning (Tina Fey) dropping dead in front of the Arconia, the same night that her new U.K.-set podcast The Girl With the Curls aired. Given the proximity, Charles, Oliver, and Mabel set out to solve her murder. “It was technically a murder in the building because she did it with her last gasp,” Hoffman told Vulture. “But the question is, what was going on with her? That is what our trio is off to investigate.”

Specific plot points will remain under wraps until its premiere, but Hoffman revealed that Charles’s Brazzos television show is getting a reboot in the U.K. “Martin Freeman will go toe-to-toe at some point with Charles as to who’s the better Brazzos,” he shared.

The show will also be taking place during the holiday season. “With the show airing at the top of December, it was impossible to avoid,” Hoffman added. “Within the story, we are moving towards the end of the year. The end of last season was fall, so we had to carry through on that.” The sixth season was shot in an uncharacteristically hot summer, but the show did its best to capture an English winter “that feels a bit rainy and a bit cloudy. There will be a bit of a lean into the holiday season by the end of it all.”

Selena Gomez, Steve Martin, and Martin Short have returned for another season. Season 5 featured newcomers Renée Zellweger and Logan Lerman, while Keegan-Michael Key was elevated to a recurring role. It’s not yet clear whether these supporting characters, along with Meryl Streep and Da’Vine Joy Randolph, will return for season 6, but we’ll stay tuned.

Season 6 has also added several notable new faces. In June 2026, Deadline reported that David Tennant, Nicola Coughlan, Jodie Whittaker, Jim Broadbent, Richard Ayoade, Adrian Lukis, and Kathryn Hunter had joined the cast in recurring roles. By August 2026, Deadline reported that Doctor Who star Peter Capaldi will also be joining the upcoming season—Whittaker, Tennant, and Broadbent have all had roles within the Doctor Who universe.

Olivia Colman was added to the cast days later, with Gomez, Streep, and Short announcing the news via an Instagram video. “We have had so many amazing guest stars for Only Murders in the Building,” Gomez said in the short clip, with Streep adding, “We’re just missing one.”

Short jokingly pops into the frame before making way for Colman to appear alongside the trio.

Colman’s character will be a writer, Hoffman revealed in the Vulture interview. “The U.K. is the home of Agatha Christie. This is where Cinda Canning might have met her maker in some way,” he said. “And all of them are following along in the tradition of the cozy murder mystery. Olivia is in that line as well as a memoirist.”

Jennifer Saunders will also have a role in the sixth season, with Hoffman telling Vulture that her character takes significant inspiration from Vivienne Westwood. “I had an incredible time many years ago working on a screenplay with Brian Grazer and Ron Howard on the life of Vivienne Westwood,” he said. “So I spent a lot of time here in London developing that screenplay with her.” Though that movie never materialized, Hoffman used his research to inform Saunders’s character, which will “lean into the fashion design world in great British style, with a tip of the hat to the great Vivienne.”

Queen Camilla was extremely close to having a role in Only Murders in the Building season 6, with Hoffman telling Vulture, “We almost got Camilla...I know it’s insane, but yes, there was a moment.”

The Queen Camilla cameo never materialized because of logistics, but Hoffman “did pursue it a little bit, until I realized it’s going to be a whole thing.” Hoffman isn’t ruling out Prince Harry and Meghan Markle cameos, though—“When is she traveling? We’re leaving in three days.” He added, “I wouldn’t rule anything out.”

Season 6 of Only Murders in the Building will arrive on December 8. Gomez and the rest of her castmates revealed that filming wrapped in an August 28 video shared to the show’s social media platforms.

Yes. First look photos arrived on August 25, and feature Gomez, Martin, and Short back in costume as Mabel, Charles, and Oliver in winter-approved wardrobe staples.

The trailer for Only Murders in the Building season 6 arrived on November 17. In the trailer, Mabel, Oliver, and Charles try to find out the truth about Cinda’s death after seeing her leave her hand on the gates of The Arconia in her final moments. “Cinda died dropping a hand on our gate,” Mabel says, to which Oliver adds, “She clearly wants our help.” After discovering that Cinda was in London just before her death, the trio travels across the pond to London with the hopes of finding out who did it.

The trio struggles with getting used to how life works in England. Simple things like drinking tea prove to be a challenge when Mabel suggests that Cinda could have died from a poisonous pot of tea. The suspects—aside from Gemma Joyce, Coughlan’s character—aren’t immediately clear, but there are a host of individuals who could be guilty of the crime as the first glimpses of Streep, Ashley, Colman, and more characters are revealed in the trailer.

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

We sniffed, we spritzed, we sprayed. Only the perfumes below left a lingering impression, earning them a spot on our annual best-of list.

Everything smells like vanilla. At least, that’s what perfume naysayers will tell you. And it’s somewhat true: If you’re judging by what’s popular, ubiquitous, or cool with the youth, then sure—vanilla scents and sweet gourmands are still trending.

Gourmands took a turn for the weird and fruity this year, with notes like lychee and rice milk popping up in otherwise innocuous formulas. Woods and ambers continued to reign, as did spice-forward and citrus scents with unexpected depth. Perfumers are meeting our demand for newness in increasingly interesting, artistic ways, and we smelled hundreds of their creations to choose the 63 winners of our annual fragrance awards.

#6860. The Slow Fade Might Actually Be Worse Than Ghosting

When someone's texts get shorter, plans become nonexistent, and you're left wondering what the hell happened.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

No one likes being rejected—unless, of course, it’s some kind of kink...in which case, more power to you. But that one-two punch to the gut is only made worse when the person you’re dating doesn’t even give you the common courtesy of honest communication.

And while I’m not going to sit here and advocate for ghosting either—in fact, quite the opposite! We’re all about the anti-ghosting movement—at least the silence provides an answer of sorts, unlike the mind fuck that is the slow fade (aka the fizzle). Unless you’re in a happy, stable relationship (heard of those?) and not dealing with the same complex dating landscape as the rest of us, you’ve likely endured this form of emotional warfare at least once. The texts come fewer and farther between, they’ve stopped initiating plans, and their responses are as stale as week-old bread. It’s a pattern of mixed communication that can lead to a lot of second-guessing.

And though we may hate to admit it to ourselves (a little delusion is fine, right? Don’t tell my therapist I said that), your situationship/casual fling/almost significant other is probably not “drowning in work” or whatever other half-assed excuse they gave you last. It’s more likely they’re just not that into you and don’t have the courage, respect, and ahem, balls to admit it.

And while you—and I, and literally any living, breathing human being—deserve a whole lot more than some emotionally incompetent clown who resorts to this form of immaturity, that doesn’t mean the slow fade isn’t hurtful and difficult to move on from. Without a concrete breakup or explanation as to what went wrong, there’s a lot of room for confusion.

“It’s much more brutal for the person on the receiving end because it leaves them with mixed signals. Rather than having information outright, they’re left to wonder what the other person is thinking, which can cause stress and anxiety and prevent them from moving on,” says licensed marriage and family therapist Emily Simonian, head of clinical learning for Thriveworks.

It’s no small epidemic either. Ghosting’s passive-aggressive cousin (as Hinge dubbed it) has become a familiar post-date communication issue for singles. According to the app’s 2023 LGBTQIA+ DATE Report, 90 percent of those surveyed said they were worried about someone “fizzling,” or slow-fading them, and it’s not just an issue in the LGBTQIA+ community. Another study by Superdrug Online Doctor found that 63 percent of participants admitted to slow-fading someone themselves. Cool cool cool.

“It’s become rampant in dating culture in response to the idea that ghosting is inappropriate or lacks compassion,” Simonian says. “However, the same individuals who might ghost due to lack of communication skills have just found a new way to avoid having difficult conversations while distancing themselves from the negative associations of ghosting.”

So while, yay, good, everyone’s finally getting the message that ghosting is not okay, the slow fade is no better. In fact, some (cough, cough me and the actual licensed therapist here) might even call it worse. It breeds confusion and, as Simonian puts it, “perpetuates the trend of avoiding difficult yet honest conversations in the dating space.” And having those difficult but honest conversations is a valuable skill you’ll need in order to make a relationship work when you’re ready. If you're the slow-fader, these negative communication habits won't exactly serve you in the long run. And they can be really destructive for the person on the receiving end, since being strung along and fed mixed messages can inspire distrust with future partners.

So what do we do to survive this hurtful dating pattern—other than the occasional, healthy cry and regular therapy? Well, for starters, if you’ve participated in the slow fade (or “fizzling,” as it’s also commonly referred to) in the past, it’s time to try a different—ahem, more mature—strategy when ending things with someone.

“The best approach is always compassionate honesty,” Simonian says. “Lead with something positive when you’re having these tough conversations, but be direct. It might be harder to let someone down directly, but it is the most compassionate way to end things.”

TL;DR: Be kind and be honest, because choosing anything but that approach is only going to make things more difficult for all parties involved. And while you can’t control another person’s actions—and as a result, you also can’t necessarily avoid the dreaded slow fade from happening to you—you can be accountable for not creating that confusion on your own end.

If your situationship is too chicken to have a real convo and is instead offering lame one-word replies, you can initiate a chat instead of just enduring (read: crying into a pint of Ben & Jerry’s) the energy shift.

“There isn’t really a way to avoid getting slow-faded,” Simonian adds. “However, you can take control of the situation and ask directly whether or not the person wants to continue dating to avoid being left in limbo.”

In other words, use the maturity they couldn’t muster to pull yourself out of the gray area. You deserve clarity, and they deserve to stew in their own discomfort for attempting to slow-fade you in the first place. Hey, a good ol’ fashioned dumping is timeless. Let’s bring that back and stop with this immature, indirect approach.

From rich burgundy to earthy olive, it’s natural to gravitate toward a dark and moody manicure as the days get shorter and cooler. But there’s a strong case to be made for embracing a lighter, fresher color in colder weather. Case in point: Jennifer Lawrence’s sun-kissed, “terracotta” nails.

#6857. Designers Reveal the Fastest Ways They've Upgraded a Kitchen Under $100

No renovation needed for these designer-approved swaps.

As you whip up a weeknight meal or prep for a dinner party, you might find yourself daydreaming—and envisioning the kitchen you wish you had. While your current cook space might be perfectly functional, it just doesn’t have the aesthetics you long for.

Alas, the kitchen is widely regarded as the most expensive room to renovate. As Houzz reports, the average cost to remodel a kitchen is currently $26,500. But if you’re happy with things like cabinets and appliances (or a renovation is far off in the future), and you want to focus on just the visuals, you can give the space a glow-up with as little as $100.

It can indeed be done, and ahead, designers are sharing their best budget-friendly upgrades.

Rugs are such thrift-able items. A good rug is basically a necessity in a kitchen, and it can easily be swapped with a new one that refreshes the visuals.

Brooke Mennen-Talsma, principal interior designer at Brooke MT Interiors, shares that in the above kitchen she designed, she added a thrifted runner that she discovered for just $40, adding a focal point to an arched pantry.

She says to not underestimate the power of secondhand, and a lot of her favorite pieces in her portfolio are thrifted items.

Switching in fresh hardware for your cabinets is something that can be done in an afternoon, and it can be gentle on the wallet, too. You can also choose to add backplates to your existing hardware, as Sherrell Neal of Sherrell Design Studio recommends. She calls it a “quick upgrade that can stay under $100,” depending on the size and number of cabinets.

While new lighting in a kitchen can quickly add up, you can add light in other, more economical ways.

For instance, as Kira Young, founder and principal designer at High Tide Design, says, you can simply go with a “cute countertop lamp with a pretty little shade.” She advises specifically purchasing a rechargeable lamp, with no messy cords and no need for an outlet. It can also be easily moved anywhere.

Homeowners are increasingly opting for collected, and even pleasantly cluttered, looks in their homes. But if you really want your kitchen visuals to look polished and pleasing, Heather Kirk, principal interior designer at Kirk Riley Design, suggests highlighting a “hero piece,” or a favorite item that shines and steals focus.

Think strong statement pieces that offer both beauty and function, “like a cheerful tea kettle on the stove or a dramatic footed bowl on the counter,” as Kirk recommends.

Emily Spanos, creative director of Emily June Designs, says that in one kitchen she designed, she painted only the lower cabinets a bright turquoise and kept the walls and upper cabinets a soft white. Paint is one of the cheapest ways to quickly upgrade a home, and it’s something that can effectively be used in the kitchen.

“Painting one element costs a fraction of painting everything, but still highlights what you want the eye to fall on,” Spanos says. “A gallon of paint and a weekend’s work keeps you under budget.”

In addition to going with lower cabinets, you could consider painting an island, pantry door, the trim, or the inside of a glass-front cabinet.

To allow your eye to have multiple things to admire in your kitchen, go with a “layered look,” as designers call it. Think: linens, decor, rugs, and artwork.

Spanos says that she has accomplished this look for under $100 in a few projects she’s worked on. In one art-filled home, she carried the creative feel into the kitchen by displaying art on a couple shelves above the sink area. In another kitchen, she laid down a black-and-white geometric rug for visual interest, which tied into the matte black hardware.

She says, “A few quality tea towels, a small rug or runner, and a piece of art can all be found affordably, especially if you shop vintage or, even better, your own home.”

#6856. Wondering How Sasha Farber Feels About Getting Blocked First on ‘The Circle’? I Flew to Atlanta to Find Out (Exclusive)

The DWTS legend tells us what he would’ve done differently on The Circle—and why he’s scared to call his parents.

Getting blocked first was not the plan. Standing in Sasha Farber’s temporary home on November 18, hours before his blocking on Hulu’s The Circle is revealed to the public, the former Dancing With the Stars pro is still in a state of disbelief. “Never in a million years did I think I was going to come on and be off first,” he tells me from his couch, where—as he threatens several times—he plans on staying until production forces him out. “On Dancing With the Stars, I think the earliest I’ve ever been eliminated was week seven.”

During his 12-season run as a pro, he’d actually been eliminated twice during week six (once with Mary Lou Retton and again with Jenn Tran), but reminding him of this would only add insult to injury. Because not only did he get eliminated first—he got blocked by DWTS superfan Alyssa Khan. Thankfully, he doesn’t hold it against her...though I’m not sure the same could be said for her fellow influencer Justin Hinsley.

Does he have any regrets? “I didn’t get to chat with the fellas, the guys. I wish I got to have a chance to speak to the guys,” he tells me. “I only spoke to Hannah [Brown] and Alyssa, and Hannah I already worked with [on DWTS]. I just wish I had that chance to build a bond with the fellas.”

But as far as how he presented himself: “I’m like, what would I do different? I don’t think I would do anything [different]. I couldn’t do anything different.”

I ask him about the first thing he’s going to do once he gets his phone back—and surprisingly, it’s not check social media (or find out who went home during the latest episode of Dancing With the Stars). “Calling mom and dad,” he says. “Oh god, I’m so scared to tell them I got eliminated.”

He adds, “People were like, ‘Oh, it’s probably going to be really hard to get offline and be in a room.’ But, I mean, look at this place. I was having the time of my life. I feel like the kitchen turned into my dance floor. I was having so much fun.”

At this point, Sasha doesn’t know yet who’s real and who’s a catfish, but when I ask him if he has any suspicions, he immediately points to Lisa Vanderpump (who, as viewers know, is being played by Vanderpump Villa star Dom DeAngelis).

“I know Lisa very well, and she doesn’t say ‘bloody hell’ very often,” he explains. “That’s like me saying, ‘Hey, guys, welcome to the Circle. Let’s put another shrimp on the barbie.’ So I was like, okay, I know Lisa is a catfish. I’m just gonna put that in my back pocket and use it when I need to. But I didn’t have a chance to use it.”

The good news? At least we’ll see Sasha in the ballroom for the Dancing With the Stars finale next week.

As he jokes, “There was so much support for me, and now I’m gonna be there for the finals, like, ‘I’m back, guys.’ You should be happy for that. The whole team is gonna be there and they’ll be like, ‘Wait, I thought you were [on The Circle],’ but it really is, like, how this show goes. You can’t really anticipate anything, which is which makes it really exciting. I don’t think there’s any show like it.”
