#6853. 40 Years On, the Musée des Arts Décoratifs’ Fashion Collection Remains Ahead of Its Time

An anniversary exhibition revisits key moments from MAD’s history

On my first trip to France in the mid-2000s, the Musée des Arts Décoratifs (MAD) became my go-to destination in Paris for a fashion-museum fix. With each subsequent visit, the intellectualized fashion scene became more saturated, as maisons increasingly invested in their heritage with public-facing venues (Fondation Azzedine Alaïa, Musée Yves Saint Laurent Paris, and Galerie Dior), plus the opening of Palais Galliera’s permanent galleries. As far as I’m concerned, when it comes to fashion exhibitions, the more the merrier. But I remain loyal to my first love. For art and design lovers like myself, it’s hard to top MAD’s interdisciplinary approach—and, of course, that prime Rue de Rivoli address.

This fall, the museum’s self-contained fashion collection—the Musée de la Mode et du Textile (originally Musée des Arts de la Mode)—celebrates its 40th anniversary with an exhibition titled “Look: 40 Years of Fashion at the Museum,” open through April 4, 2027. While the museum’s central hall, the Nef (“nave”), pays homage to 40 of its 70-plus shows by spotlighting a look to represent each, the surrounding galleries invite visitors behind the scenes of an atelier and the museum itself.

“As the custodian of France’s National Fashion Collection, it was really important for us to show people their heritage, as well as the diversity of the collection,” Sophie Lemahieu, the museum’s curator of fashion and textile collections after 1947, told Harper’s Bazaar. Co-curating the exhibition with MAD director Bénédicte Gady, Lemahieu sought to convey the skills of the many professionals who made each garment a reality, as well as the museum workers who care for and prepare them for display.

The multilayered approach represents a consecration of fashion as art—a concept MAD’s Musée de la Mode et du Textile has been instrumental in transmitting since its inception, when such a notion was far from the prevailing opinion. “40 years ago, it was not obvious to have fashion in the museum, even in Paris. There were many curators and art historians who were extremely mad because all these floors could have been used to show Islamic art or other decorative art collections,” said Lemahieu. “It took time to legitimize fashion history as an art history field, but now every museum wants to have fashion exhibitions because they’ve seen how meaningful they can be. People identify with fashion because it’s part of our lives. We all wear clothes.”

For context, MAD was founded in 1864 and contained textiles and fashion prior to the official establishment of the Musée des Arts de la Mode. The decision to formalize its fashion collection was twofold: first, it marked the union of two monumental collections, the Union Centrale des Arts Décoratifs and the Union Française des Arts de la Costume. More broadly, during the 1980s, under President François Mitterrand, the French government began advocating for fashion as national cultural prestige, rather than a purely commercial entity (though it was undeniably one of the country’s leading exports). In 1982, the Louvre’s Cour Carrée courtyard became a designated venue for French ready-to-wear shows, making a powerful global statement. To quote Lemahieu, “Fashion became political.”

For the Musée des Arts de la Mode’s inaugural exhibition, “Moments de Mode” (1986)—which in “Look” is illustrated by a pristine 1760 robe à la française, the oldest garment in the show—MAD pulled out all the scenography stops. Working with theater professionals, curators presented an immersive history of fashion, including a mannequin, clad in a 19th-century cycling suit, in continuous movement on a mechanical bicycle. While today, such a display would never happen due to conservation requirements, it set the tone for MAD’s myriad innovations showcasing garments at their finest.

Another fascinating example from Moments de Mode is MAD’s usage of invisible mounts, which make garments appear as if they are floating. Visitors to Fondation Azzedine Alaïa may recall that these spectral mannequins have become somewhat of a signature for the institution. Lemahieu said this is not a coincidence: “Mr. Alaïa actually came to the exhibition in 1986 and thought this was an absolutely wonderful way to show a dress, so he asked the same person to do the mannequins for his foundation.”

Elsewhere are odes to MAD’s many monographic shows, from its first, Yves Saint Laurent in 1986, to its traveling blockbuster “Christian Dior, Couturier of Dreams” in 2017. Dior is one of the few designers to have multiple MAD exhibitions—another is Schiaparelli, who was the subject of shows in 2004 and 2022. Similarly, MAD staged two exhibitions about the 1980s—in 1989 and 2022. Lemahieu noted in all cases, however, MAD never repeats its approach to the same subject.

She also pointed to several exhibitions conceived by her colleague, Denis Bruna, curator in chief of the Fashion and Textile Department, who favors a more sociological approach. “The Mechanics of Undergarments: An Indiscreet History of the Silhouette” (2013), for example, explored techniques used by women and men to shape their bodies, while “Proper Attire Required: When Clothing Causes a Scandal” (2016), focused on controversies that marked turning points in fashion history.

Nearly all the garments in “Look” were previously featured in their corresponding exhibitions. The one exception is a tricolor bodysuit that Gilles Asquin created for the 2024 Paris Olympics Opening Ceremony (several young designers contributed looks for the momentous event). The garment, which the French National Olympic and Sports Committee entrusted to MAD, is displayed to represent the exhibition “Fashion and Sport, From One Podium to Another,” 2023. Lemahieu said the bodysuit’s inclusion demonstrates how museum exhibitions can open pathways for future donations.

Acquisition, documentation, storage, conservation, and mannequinage (the art of mounting a garment) are some of the internal museum processes discussed in the “How Clothing Becomes a Work of Art: Backstage at the Museum” galleries. Meanwhile, “Backstage: Creating Couture” focuses on inspiration, fabrication, ornamentation, and photography. It’s here where MAD can proudly show the range of its holdings (more than 150,000 pieces from the third century to the present day), which include preparatory sketches, fashion illustrations, embroidery samples, and more. Rarities include a mannequin upon which Madeleine Vionnet draped her fabrics, as well as pleating boards used by Maison Lognon, a historic Parisian pleating atelier founded in 1853.

Running concurrently this fall at MAD are exhibitions dedicated to the Thai court’s fashion evolution, Brazilian photographer Rafael Pavarotti’s cutting-edge imagery, and Marion Delarue’s wearable art created during her residency at the museum. Forty years on, this mix of shows, in scale and subject, has become MAD’s unique strength, as Lemahieu described: “It’s important for us to not just make bigger and bigger exhibitions. We need to be involved in what happens in haute couture, but we also need to be here for young designers and to be aware of what’s happening in fashion socially.” It’s that distinctive cocktail of programming—not to mention all the period-room interiors, jewels, and posters—that still keeps MAD at the top of my Paris museum list after all these years.

#6846. Before & After: Two Best Friends Turned a Stripped-Down Tudor Into a Colorful, Antique-Filled Dream Home

Designer Anne Hulcher Tollett understood her client’s signature aesthetic better than anyone.

Anne Hulcher Tollett can’t remember a life without her best friend, Van. They met in a playpen as toddlers and quickly became an integral part of each other's lives, with many childhood memories taking place in Van’s house growing up. Tollett has been with her best friend through every milestone, including as her maid of honor at her wedding to her husband, Sean, and when the couple put an offer on a suburbanized Tudor home in Richmond, Virginia.

The five-bedroom house had good bones and an intuitive flow, but it needed help getting some of its charm back. When Van and Sean bought it for their family of four (plus their dog, Jelly Bean, and a small flock of chickens), it was whitewashed and lacked architectural character. In short, it wasn’t up to par with Van’s eclectic style, according to Tollett, but as the principal designer of Hanover Avenue, she reassured her bestie that she could easily take it from suburban chic to warm, layered, and personality-filled without having to completely rework the interior.

“Van’s aesthetic is diametrically opposed to ‘simple’ or ‘builder-grade,’ and although this house had been meticulously renovated and maintained by the previous owners, some of the charm she loves had been removed,” says Tollett. The designer and her team focused on lighter renovations and cosmetic upgrades, walking her best friend through every decision, from wallpaper choices to reworking the staircase to highlight the amazing treasures Van has collected on her travels over the years.

Throughout the renovation, Tollett and Van leaned into their sisterly shorthand, resulting in a project with very few disruptions. “Our biggest challenge was creating unexpected quirky moments without taking down walls or undoing all of the thoughtful and well-built upgrades that came with the house,” Tollett explains. “We found places on tables, walls, and bookshelves to highlight everything Van and her family have collected over the years. It makes the house feel like theirs and not the product of some design firm.”

Walls were covered with grasscloth, patterns were layered on throw pillows and vintage rugs, and anything that wasn’t antique or pre-owned was chosen to add a touch of patina to the space, allowing Van’s collections to truly sing.

Once Tollett was finished with the renovation, the house told the story of their friendship and a life well lived. Because the designer knows Van like the back of her hand, she was able to create a retreat that feels like a true reflection of who she is and how her family lives without editing out any of Van’s eccentricities. “We understood her design sensibility and took something well-built but fairly plain and made it fun, pretty, and interesting at every turn, which is exactly how anyone would describe Van!” Tollett adds. Besides, who better to design your house than the person who knows you better than yourself?

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne Hulcher Tollett of Hanover AvenueLocation: Richmond, VirginiaThe Space: A 4,600-square-foot Tudor home with five bedrooms, four full bathrooms, and two half-baths

The barrel back and turquoise chairs were finds that Tollett sourced for Van’s previous home, proving that timeless profiles and accessible sizes can work in many different spaces throughout their lifetimes.

“A room like this with a vaulted ceiling and white walls creates a wonderful backdrop for bold pops of color and patterns because it can visually hold its own against furnishings with personality,” she says. “Van had the beautiful sideboard, and we loved pairing it with the tattooed table.”

Possibly one of the most remarkable makeovers in the entire house, Tollett coated the entryway in deep blue paint, topping it off with darling wallpaper on the ceiling for added dimension. “This is a great example of how a can of paint, a roll of wallpaper, and some strategically placed treasures can transform a room,” she says.

Large-scale grasscloth wallpaper instantly gave the once-all-white box character and rhythm, setting a serene backdrop for the rest of the unique finds in this dining room. The chandelier hanging over the table had long been on Tollett’s list to use in a project, and Van’s home was the perfect spot.

“The dining room is right near the front door, so it’s hung to look like it's being blown by the entrance breeze—a true classic with a twist!” the designer says.

Above the antique bar (whose natural patina plays so well with the wallpaper), are two framed photos of puppets, which Van found during one of her globe-trotting trips. She carried them around in her backpack, and Tollett loved the story behind them so much that she had them framed. On the opposite wall, behind the piano, is a portrait of Van’s grandmother in a vibrant green dress.

The kitchen was well-maintained when Tollett got her hands on it, but it didn’t feel all that fun, and it needed more than simple cosmetic refreshes like other areas of the home.

“We removed the big-box island lighting and reworked the storage, hood, and casework on the range wall,” Tollett explains. “We did the same on the wall opposite the range,” she adds, which was in fantastic shape, so they kept it.

To give the cook space more character, the designer installed a custom Delftware tile backsplash. The island was also in good shape, so Tollett upgraded the hardware, faucet, face frames, and lighting above it, opting for three Pinch pendant lights which bring an interesting, natural texture to the space.

“Open concept kitchens are tricky because they are seen from every angle, so we knew we needed to improve the storage to hide clutter,” she adds. Opposite the range wall, Tollett removed the open shelves, creating closed casework instead and topping everything off with pretty hardware and colors that play well with the adjacent dining and living rooms.

Tollett knew she was going to wrap this powder room in a beautiful butterfly-and-bird wallpaper, so she custom-matched a paint to fully drench the room in a whimsical emerald green hue. They even had their wonderful wallpaper installers cut out a few extra birds and butterflies to extend the design onto the ceiling, creating a more immersive experience. “We wanted the powder room to pack an equal punch to the bold, blue entryway it connects to,” she explains.

One of the mantras Tollett and her team at Hanover Avenue follow is “wonderfully weird,” which the hand sculpture extending through the wall to hold the soap perfectly captures. “The space feels not only more fun but also much more personalized to our clients!”

During the renovation, Van’s mother passed away, so Tollett transformed this almost-forgotten area into an homage to the matriarch. “We reused her beautiful plate rack and hung all her flawless collections of plates on the walls,” the designer says, officially turning this room, which overlooks the vegetable gardens, into the “plate room.”

They papered the walls in warm neutral grasscloth and hung relaxed Roman shades to softly filter the natural light, furnishing the room with many of the fabulous pieces Van already owned (and loved). “This is a wonderful example of ‘reimagining what you have,’ and finding ways to remember the ones we love in our own rooms,” Tollett adds. “Van’s mom was the coolest!”

“Decades of sleepovers in Van’s childhood bedroom allowed me to execute exactly what she was hoping to achieve,” Tollett explains. Though there are many layers of pattern in this sitting area, the designer managed to prevent it from feeling busy by keeping the same serene color palette for most of the textiles, minus a few pops of red for a warm balance.

“This room is a grown-up translation of Van’s entire design sensibility that began when she was young and was nurtured in her adulthood during her travels,” Tollett adds.

On the other side of the room is a calming wash of warm white paint and natural textures, giving Van a visual place of rest ideal for sleeping. Tollett calls it a “well-collected, well-traveled, layered space that seems to have evolved all on its own—a sanctuary for Van and Sean,” which makes the designer especially happy since she was the maid of honor at the couple’s wedding.

The primary bath really only needed some cosmetic upgrades, but they did a lot of heavy lifting. Airy Roman shades soften the space while Van’s mother’s bench and one of her existing rugs give it personality. By opting for two warmer paint colors and swapping out key fixtures, such as the lights, hardware, and faucets, Tollett created a serene, sophisticated escape that looks completely new, all with the same bones.

Tollett tapped into her childhood memories with her best friend when designing this room. “During slumber parties, Van and I fell asleep across from each other in twin beds, so it felt fitting to create another bedroom that fosters the same kind of childhood chatter,” she says, adding that stepping inside is like stepping into a time machine going back to 1983.

Along with the beds, much of the look was directly inspired by Van’s childhood bedroom, the round green pillow on the bed having been plucked directly from it. The pre-existing Tudor windows were perfect for the vintage vibe.

Tucked away at the far end of the home is a spot connected to the sunroom lounge. Dubbed the “dayroom”—an informal spot dedicated to relaxation and reading—Tollett wanted it to feel cozy and as collected as the rest of the home but still a bit private. They washed the walls in a soft gray-green hue and did the same to the custom day bed, finishing off the room with antique bookcases in a complementary gray tone. It’s easy for small rooms like these to feel overcrowded, but the pale color palette takes away some visual weight.

As founder and principal designer of Hanover Avenue, Anne Hulcher Tollett is intimately involved in every project the Richmond-based design firm takes on, from high-end residential to boutique commercial. Tollett finds a thrill in uncovering her clients’ design styles and endless joy in creating their dream homes that reflect who they are.

#6845. Why We Need to Stop Saying “I’m Sorry” When Someone Breaks Up

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

When my last relationship ended, I felt better than I had in months—maybe even years. If I’m being honest, I was probably happier after we split than I was when we first got together, and it made me feel like the heartless Bachelor villain of my own love life.

It’s not like I’d planned or even expected this post-breakup bliss. Like literally every other person who has ever been in a relationship, I wasn’t exactly looking forward to the end of mine. But when it finally happened, I was relieved—dare I say, excited? It wasn’t even like I had been actively miserable in the relationship; to this day, I’m not even sure whether it’s fair to say it exceeded standard toxicity levels. But I did know it had run its course, and I was grateful to have my future back, one I wouldn’t have to spend trying to convince myself a good-enough relationship was, in fact, good enough.

But to the rest of the world, this largely painless ending appeared to be a tragedy. Anyone I mentioned the split to, from friends and family to random strangers and acquaintances, immediately responded with some version of “I’m sorry.” In response, I’d find myself explaining that actually I was fine and the breakup was very much for the best. Unfortunately, this had a way of making me come off like a heartbroken caricature of a delusional woman desperately trying to convince everyone she’s fine when really I…actually was fine!

Being on the receiving end of all these condolences had me wondering if I actually was delusional—or maybe just dead inside. Had one too many heartbreaks, ghostings, and life-altering situationships rendered me fully incapable of mourning my own relationship the way everyone else seemed to be doing?

Exhausted from absorbing all this well-intentioned, if unsolicited, sympathy, I started trying to avoid mentioning the breakup altogether. Until one day, a few weeks after the split, when I casually broke the news to a friend at a party and she said, “Congratulations!”

I was so thrilled, so relieved to finally get a response from someone that actually matched my own feelings, I could’ve wept tears of actual joy. Maybe my lack of grief and I weren’t the problem! Maybe the problem I kept brushing up against with every unsolicited “sorry” had more to do with the patriarchal ideals our society still upholds but which I had recently rejected—the ones that dictate that being partnered, however happily or unhappily, is always superior to being single, especially if you’re a woman.

To be clear, I’m not saying that expressing sympathy for what may very well be an emotionally painful experience makes anyone rude or careless or insufficiently evolved. But the default “I’m sorry” most of us reach for when confronted with breakup news paints the dissolution of a relationship as a universal loss, reinforcing the dated idea that being partnered is paradise and being single is sad.

“There are a lot of societal, cultural, or even personal projections made when we automatically assume someone is devastated after a breakup,” explains therapist Lindsey Brock, founder of the Breakup Therapist. “It’s never helpful to assume what someone is thinking or how they are responding emotionally to any situation.”

And sometimes, the breakup is actually good news. “It’s natural for people to say ‘I’m sorry’ because they associate breakups with heartache and want to express sympathy,” says Shannon Lundgren, founder of the matchmaking service Shannon's Circle and author of the upcoming book The Love Funnel. “It’s not the most helpful response because it assumes the person is heartbroken, when they may feel relieved or happy about the ending.”

Perhaps more important than whatever heteropatriarchal norms we may or may not be reinforcing when we apologize to someone for their own breakup is the simple fact that “I’m sorry” might not be an appropriate response to a given situation. This default apology ignores the reality that, in many cases, a breakup might be totally neutral, or even something to celebrate.

“Walking away from a relationship may be the most courageous yet difficult thing someone does,” adds Brock. “Meeting them with an ‘I'm sorry’ may undermine that bravery, or even leave someone second-guessing whether they made the right decision.”

While the end of a relationship certainly may be cause for sympathy—I myself have had my share of emotionally annihilating splits in which condolences were very much in order—it’s not always appropriate or helpful to automatically assume that someone is struggling just because they’ve been through a breakup.

“Culturally, we are conditioned to assume that when a relationship ends, it’s a negative thing and that it must therefore be an unfortunate event,” says relationship coach Natalia Juarez, founder of Lovistics. “While expressing sympathy in some situations is appropriate, it’s essential to consider a variety of factors. Each breakup is unique and multi-faceted.” Rather than reaching for a canned response, “it’s far better to actively listen and be mindful,” says Juarez.

It's also worth remembering that even the person who initiated the breakup might be struggling. “People assume that if you did the breaking up, that you aren't unhappy about it, but that's not always true,” adds Susan Trombetti, matchmaker and CEO of Exclusive Matchmaking.

This, of course, requires a little more effort than offering the standard post-breakup condolences. Below, a few tips from the pros for approaching breakup news more mindfully.

Don’t assume you know how someone is feeling. An easy way to avoid doing this? Just ask! Instead of reaching for an “Oh, I’m so sorry,” Juarez recommends starting out with a simple “How are you feeling about it?”

Another option? “Do you want to talk about it, or talk about something else?” suggests Lundgren. It's a simple way to let someone decide whether they're in the mood to unpack the breakup or would rather focus on literally anything else.

“As a general rule, it’s most supportive to ask how someone is feeling about a big transition. This gives them room to honor their different feelings and experiences without the pressure to fit some social norm or expectation,” says Brock. “They may feel terrified yet hopeful, sad and relieved, guilty yet proud, ecstatic and free.”

It's also worth recognizing the courage it can take to end a relationship. “Everyone thinks breakups are awful, but that misses how much bravery it takes to make that decision,” notes Vanessa Errecarte, author of Valuable & Visible.

Sometimes the best thing you can give someone going through something significant is space to embrace what may be a wide range of conflicting emotions.

But know that that might look very different from situation to situation. Before expressing sympathy or offering advice, try asking something along the lines of “How can I support you?” says Brock. “Sometimes people need someone to celebrate with them, distract them from misery, give sound advice, plan an adventure, or join them in bashing their ex. When in doubt, just listen and never assume.”

Support doesn't always have to involve a conversation about the breakup, either. “Inviting someone out to lunch or dinner shows you care,” says Trombetti. “Just sitting with them is very comforting and shows caring and empathy.”

Asking someone what, if anything, they need instead of assuming makes it much easier to provide actually useful support to a friend who might need it. “Communicating openly is so much better than simply saying, ‘If you need me, I’m here,’” explains Juarez. “The fact is, many broken-hearted people don’t reach out because they don’t want to be a burden.”

A little self-reflection never hurts. When confronted with the news of someone else’s split, it may be “helpful to assess and challenge your own beliefs about marriage or long-term partnership,” says Brock. “While it’s totally okay to want a long-term partnership for yourself, others may not share that as a priority. As you support your friend through their breakup, stay mindful that being single isn’t inherently bad or wrong or just a blip on the radar en route to partnership.”

Instead of treating the end of a relationship as a setback, consider acknowledging the strength it can take to walk away from something that isn't working. “Help the person who's going through the breakup see the strength in making a hard decision,” says Errecarte.

#6843. Frog Closures Are the Unexpected Detail Defining Fall Jackets

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

It’s all in the details, from intricate knots to elaborate braiding

There are plenty of ways to make a statement with your outerwear, from an oversize silhouette to a high collar or bold color. But this fall, designers are turning their attention to a smaller detail: the fastening. Jackets featuring decorative knots, elaborate loops, and ornate toggles have emerged as one of the season’s biggest outerwear trends, offering an alternative to standard buttons and zippers.

In particular, frog fastenings are having a resurgence, bringing a fanciful finishing touch to everything from minimalist wool peacoats to tailored military-inspired jackets. Typically constructed from fabric or braided cord, the fasteners consist of a knotted button and a loop. Originally designed to secure garments, the closures have evolved into a more ornamental role with intricate shapes and floral-like designs.

While they may seem new for the season, frog-fastener jackets have a history that spans centuries. In Chinese dress, knotted closures known as pankou have long appeared on garments such as the qipao and tangzhuang. By the 18th and 19th centuries, frogging had also become a feature of European military uniforms, worn by cavalry regiments whose fitted jackets were adorned with braided cords.

Fashion has revisited the detail since then. Oscar de la Renta incorporated frog fastenings into his designs in the 1990s, while Valentino explored versions for its fall 2015 couture collection. More recently, for fall 2026, designers reinterpreted knotted closures: At Kallmeyer, black toggles punctuated a gilded jacquard jacket, while Kim Shui incorporated the detail into fur-trimmed blazers and opera jackets. Meanwhile, labels like Khaite and Ann Demeulemeester embraced military-inspired looks featuring ornamental braiding and embellished fronts.

Today’s store-rack versions range from collarless blazers with understated knots to dramatic outerwear designs featuring detailed cording. Brands like Róhe have introduced contemporary takes on frogging, while Hong Kong–based fashion label Sau Lee has reimagined traditional Tang-style jackets with modern silhouettes.

Rather than reinventing jackets entirely, designers are proving that something as minuscule as a fastening can make a familiar silhouette feel new again for the season. Ahead, shop our favorite takes on the look.

#6841. Asking for Rough Sex Is All About Being Specific

Below, sex experts explain how to bring it up, set boundaries, and explore rougher sex safely.

Maybe you're into the idea of having your wrists pinned above your head. Maybe a little hair-pulling or spanking sounds hot. Or maybe you've been thinking about asking your partner to be a little rougher in bed, but you're not entirely sure how to bring it up without making things awkward. Whatever your version of rough sex looks like, figuring out how to ask for it can feel surprisingly intimidating.

For one thing, what does “rougher” even mean? Are we talking a firmer grip during sex? A little spanking? Being told what to do? The possibilities are pretty much endless, and what sounds hot to one person might be a hard no for someone else.

“That's why ‘Do you like it rough?’ is just the start of a conversation. Ask what ‘rough’ actually means to the person you're with,” says Lee Anne Wine, a Toronto-based certified sex therapist and educator.

Of course, knowing what you want and actually saying it out loud are two different things. And while asking your partner to try something new can feel vulnerable, getting specific about your desires, boundaries, and expectations is what makes exploring rough sex possible without anyone having to guess what the other person is into.

Whether you're curious about BDSM or just want to experiment with a little more intensity, here's how to bring it up, figure out what you're both comfortable with, and get started.

Rough sex is exactly what it sounds like: consensual sex that involves a little more physical intensity than you might be used to. That could mean anything from firmer kissing and gripping to spanking, hair-pulling, or being more assertive with your partner. And while rough sex can overlap with BDSM, the two aren't necessarily interchangeable.

“Rough sex is more spontaneous intensity woven into regular sex,” explains Annette Benedetti, sex and intimacy coach and host of Talk Sex With Annette. BDSM, on the other hand, can involve more structured activities, negotiated power dynamics, and roles like dominant and submissive. You can enjoy one, both, or neither.

As for why people are into it? For some, it's about heightened physical sensations. For others, the appeal is more psychological, whether that's taking control, giving it up, or simply feeling desired in a different way. The important distinction is that everyone involved actively wants what's happening, and that consent can change at any time.

Asking your partner for rougher sex doesn't require a perfectly rehearsed speech. You just need to be honest about what you're curious about and willing to hear what your partner thinks, too.

As tempting as it might be to blurt out “Be rougher!” in the middle of sex, it's better to have the initial conversation when you're both fully clothed and not distracted by everything else going on.

“Bring it up with your clothes on, not in the heat of the moment, and lead with curiosity instead of a demand,” says Benedetti. “Something like, ‘I’ve been curious about trying something a little more intense; would you be open to talking about it?’”

The key is making it an invitation rather than implying your partner isn't doing enough for you in bed. You can even admit you're nervous. Saying something like “This feels a little awkward to bring up, but I've been thinking about trying something new with you” gives you a way into the conversation without putting pressure on either of you to agree to anything right away.

And if your partner needs time to think about it? That's fine, too. The point is to start talking, not get an immediate yes.

Here's the thing about asking someone to be rougher: You're going to have to explain what that actually means to you. Because “rough” could mean a firmer grip on your hips, being held down, a little spanking, or something else entirely.

Wine recommends starting with one specific example rather than trying to describe an entire fantasy at once. You might say, “I feel a little shy saying this, but I think I’d enjoy it if you held my hips a little more firmly when we kiss. How does that sound to you?”

From there, talk about what you each find appealing and what you're not interested in trying. Maybe you like the idea of being told what to do but have zero interest in pain. Maybe you're curious about spanking but not restraints. None of those preferences need to come as a package deal.

And remember, asking for something doesn't mean your partner has to be into it, just as their interest in something doesn't mean you have to try it. You're figuring out where your desires overlap, not negotiating someone into a yes.

Before you start experimenting, get clear about what's on the table, what's a definite no, and what you might be open to exploring later. That includes talking about intensity, where you do and don't want to be touched, and whether either of you has injuries or sensitivities to consider.

“If you are planning to try something new, such as spanking, impact play, biting, or scratching, make sure to discuss your intentions first with your partner,” says Candice Smith, sex expert and cofounder of The KinkKit.

It can help to make a simple yes/maybe/no list together so neither of you has to guess. You'll also want to agree on a safeword or signal before things get started. One option is the traffic-light system: “green” means you're comfortable continuing, “yellow” means pause or slow down and check in, and “red” means stop immediately.

Wine also recommends having a nonverbal signal, such as three taps, in case speaking becomes difficult. And regardless of what safeword you choose, regular words like “no” and “stop” always count. Consent isn't something you establish once and then forget about. Either person can change their mind at any point, even if you've already agreed to try something.

Once you've talked through what you're both interested in trying, you can start experimenting. But rougher doesn't automatically mean better, and there's no reason to jump straight into the most intense version of something you've never done before. A little trial and error is part of figuring out what works for you, as long as you're both communicating along the way.

If you're new to rough sex, you don't need to break out the handcuffs on night one. In fact, you don't need any equipment at all. Try adding a firmer grip around your partner's waist while kissing, experimenting with a more assertive tone during dirty talk, or asking them to guide your hands during sex.

Wine recommends starting with something you already enjoy and changing just one detail. That way, you can figure out what feels good without introducing a bunch of new sensations at once.

You can also experiment with intensity using a simple rating system. For example, start with gentle pressure and ask your partner to rate it on a scale of one to five, then adjust based on their feedback. Just remember that what feels like a three to you might feel like a five to someone else.

If spanking is on your list of things to try, start gently. Impact play involves consensual hitting or spanking for sensation, and knowing where and how to make contact matters.

“If you’ve both agreed to spanking, start with light, open-handed contact on the fleshy part of the buttocks. Keep impact away from the spine, lower back, and tailbone,” advises Wine.

Avoid assuming that harder is automatically hotter. Start with low intensity, check in frequently, and only increase it if your partner explicitly wants you to. And if either of you decides you're not into it after all, that's a perfectly good reason to stop.

Hair-pulling and restraints might sound relatively straightforward, but both can cause injuries if you're not careful.

For hair-pulling, Wine recommends agreeing on a gentle hold close to the scalp rather than yanking on the ends or jerking someone's head backward. If you're curious about bondage, start with soft cuffs that have a quick-release mechanism you've already tested.

Skip zip ties, cords, and improvised restraints that can tighten unexpectedly. Never leave someone restrained alone, and release them immediately if they experience pain, numbness, tingling, or changes in skin color.

And one particularly important safety note: Keep pressure off the neck and avoid anything that restricts breathing. As Wine puts it, “There’s no safe way to choke someone during sex.”

Rougher sex doesn't mean you need to deal with uncomfortable friction. In fact, adding some lube can make a big difference, particularly if you're experimenting with more vigorous penetration.

Keep some extra lube nearby, reapply as needed, and pay attention to how your body feels. If something starts to hurt or feel irritating, don't try to push through it. Just make sure you're using a lubricant that's compatible with any condoms or sex toys involved. Oil-based lubricants, for example, can damage latex condoms.

Even if you've already discussed your boundaries and agreed on a safeword, that doesn't mean you can stop communicating once things get going.

Wine suggests keeping check-ins simple with questions like “More, less, or same?” or “Do you want me to keep going?” That way, you're giving your partner an easy opportunity to tell you what feels good and what doesn't.

And don't rely on verbal responses alone. If someone suddenly goes quiet, freezes, pulls away, or seems disconnected, stop what you're doing and check in rather than assuming they're enjoying themselves.

“If something hurts, feels wrong, or goes past a boundary, stop the activity and release any restraints immediately,” Wine advises. “Ask what they need and give them room to answer. Nobody owes an explanation for stopping. Only resume if you both clearly want to.”

Just because the sex is over doesn't mean the experience is. Aftercare, aka taking care of each other physically and emotionally after an intense sexual experience, is an important part of exploring rough sex. And what that looks like will depend on the people involved.

“Someone might want water and a blanket, a hug, reassurance, or a few minutes alone,” says Wine. “Ask before assuming touch will help.”

For some people, aftercare might mean cuddling and talking about what they loved. For others, it could mean grabbing a snack, tending to any sore spots, or having a little space to themselves. There's no one right way to do it, and what feels comforting to one person might not be what another needs.

It's also worth checking in with each other the next day, especially if you're trying something new. Did you enjoy the intensity? Was there anything that surprised you? Is there something you'd want to do differently next time?

As Benedetti puts it, “There’s no award for intensity.” Rough sex isn't about pushing yourself or your partner to see how far you can go. It's about figuring out what you both enjoy, respecting each other's limits, and knowing you can always decide something isn't for you.

#6836. The Press-On Nails That Made Me Quit Nail Salons

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As a chronic nail biter (gross, I know), I’ve tried just about every type of manicure out there in an attempt to heal my nails and kick my habit. From acrylics and tips to builder gel and dip powder, nothing has ever worked for me. But one simple option has never let me down: press-ons. They provide a natural-looking manicure without damaging your nails, take just minutes to apply, and can be done from the comfort of your own home. There’s no drying, intense filing, or pricey appointments required. What’s not to love?

Recently, I fell down a glue-filled rabbit hole in search of the best press-on nails out there. Every week, I’d apply a new set in the name of research, meticulously assessing each for quality, durability, and longevity. I have very small nail beds, so finding a size, shape, and length that fit my fingertips was essential. After about a year of testing (and an absurd amount of nail glue), there was a clear winner: Glamnetic.

We praised the product last year, and that praise still holds true. The application is not only simple, but I actually find it quite therapeutic. I start by pushing back my cuticles with the included wooden stick and filing the entire surface of the nails, not just the edges. Doing this helps the press-ons adhere better and last longer. Next, I match each of my fingers to the best-fitting press-on. Finally, I remove any oils from my nails using the alcohol pad, and glue on the set. I start with my pinkies and move toward my thumbs, placing a dot of glue at the base of each press-on and holding it down for about 30 to 45 seconds. Before I know it, I’m left with a perfect manicure that’s affordable, natural-looking, and long-lasting.

While it’s always fun to experiment with different shapes and lengths, my personal favorite is the brand’s “Short Oval” collection. For under $20, each set comes with 30 nails in varying sizes, and all of the above materials. They are incredibly durable without looking or feeling thick, and each set lasts about two weeks.

Whether you prefer a milky pink shade like Strawberry Icing or a more festive set like Snowfall, Glamnetic is known for its impressive designs. From magnetic cat-eye polish to 3-D chrome appliqués, there’s a set for every style, season, and mood. For a nail biter who has destroyed just about every expensive manicure imaginable, finding a budget-friendly option that can actually keep up with me feels like a small miracle.

#6834. Take Note: This Is How Cool People Are Decorating for Thanksgiving This Year

These tastemakers are bringing their personal style to the holiday.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pumpkin centerpieces, rustic garlands, dried-fruit wreaths, and cornucopias are classic Thanksgiving decorations. They’re timeless and beloved, but they can also feel predictable. Traditional decor has its place, of course, but Thanksgiving tablescapes—and dinner parties in general—are evolving, much like interiors, to feel more personal, lived-in, and layered. So we asked nine tastemakers and experts how they’re decorating for this family-centric holiday.

From traditional touches to wholly unexpected looks, read on to see how they’re making their homes feel festive this autumn, and maybe you’ll be inspired to try something new this year.

Ever since designer Mikel Welch moved to sunny California, his family has never hesitated to go to his for Thanksgiving to escape the chilly Michigan weather. It’s become a tradition to gather at his house, and similarly to how he grew up, Welch likes to keep his decorations for the holiday understated and warm.

“I love getting the exterior of my house into full fall mode with mums, pumpkins, and seasonal greenery,” Welch tells House Beautiful. “The beauty of fall decorating is that it can carry you from October straight through Thanksgiving without having to change much.”

The designer’s house is filled with dark, moody colors and earthy textures year-round, so he focuses mainly on bringing natural elements inside to do the work for him. “I also have a little tradition with my dad,” he adds. “For the past couple of years, we've made trips to the nursery together to pick out flowers, greenery, and sometimes bales of hay for the front of the house. It's become something I really enjoy doing with him.”

“Thanksgiving has always been a big family affair,” real estate expert and maximalism extraordinaire Harvin Greene says. She’s happy to keep that tradition going as an adult, just in her own way. In the past, Greene’s aunt and uncle would have everyone at their house for the big meal, but last year, they passed the hosting baton to Greene for the first time. “I may have gotten a little carried away...in the best possible way.”

Getting to decorate two tables—one for the “adults,” another for the “kids,” even though they’re technically all grown-ups, too—Greene used this opportunity to design two completely different tablescapes. “I grew up surrounded by women who understood that a beautifully set table and a genuinely good time should go hand in hand,” she adds.

The one in the formal dining space (i.e., the “adults’” table) was theatrical and dramatic, and nearly everything on it had some sort of familial connection, from her parents’ engraved goblets to her husband’s grandmother’s sterling flatware. The kids’ table, on the other hand, was whimsical and vibrant, complete with a yellow patterned tablecloth, colorful glassware, and playful animal candlesticks. “I don't feel the need to follow the traditional Thanksgiving color palette,” Greene says. “Who decided Thanksgiving had to be orange and brown? Yellow, pink, turquoise, and green can be just as festive.”

The holiday isn’t over until they bring out the Bingo cards, a spirited Thanksgiving tradition that Greene’s Aunt Jean started—which involves cash prizes. “It's one of those family traditions that makes the holiday feel like ours, no matter whose house we're celebrating in.”

Talia Hubble is no stranger to a good dinner party—much of her content actually centers around her love of hosting. But Thanksgiving is a bit extra special for the Rhode Islander, especially since it’s being held at her cottage this year. “Growing up, it was just me and my mom, and she was too busy to decorate,” Hubble says. “I think that’s part of why I’ve fallen in love with seasonal decorating as an adult, I get to bring all my cozy childhood decorating dreams to life!”

As soon as the calendar switches from October to November, Hubble starts decorating for fall, opting for an eclectic mix of subtle Christmas decor (think bows, garlands, and wreaths), an excessive amount of taper candles, and a traditional Thanksgiving palette of oranges, browns, greens, and reds. Every year looks a little bit different, but there are pieces in her collection that are simply too cute not to bring out every holiday season.

“Half the fun of pulling everything out for Thanksgiving is remembering where it came from,” she says. “I think the best decor is a culmination of pieces you’ve been collecting for years, and the memories behind those pieces are what make the table feel special and uniquely yours.”

By the time she’s done decorating, her home feels like a warm, welcoming escape, somewhere her guests can exhale. “My philosophy is that you can’t go wrong when you aim for cozy, colorful, and embrace a little organized mess,” Hubble adds. “My favorite Thanksgiving tables are the ones that look even better halfway through dinner, when the napkins are rumpled and everyone’s reaching for seconds.”

Chef and food stylist Romilly Newman can’t always count on being in the same place as her family for Thanksgiving since everyone’s scattered about, but she always makes a point to throw an autumnal dinner with friends around the holiday time, complete with beautiful fall decor.

Her mom never decorated their home while she was growing up with specific Thanksgiving decorations—instead, she would decorate with deep orange and red flowers to capture the fall vibe. Newman’s style as an adult is similar. “I am fall-obsessed, so as soon as the temperature drops from summer, I'm setting up branches, candles, and darker accents,” she says. “I love the atmosphere inside and outside around Thanksgiving time, and I try my best to bottle that up in my home.”

Natural thicker linen, pops of silver, dark florals, and moody lighting envelop her space as the chef relies mostly on candlelight and organic elements—like figs, sage, chestnuts, and mandarins—as the decorative accents.

“Thanksgiving is great because it brings family together and offers time to enjoy food, friends, and being at home,” Newman adds. “Whatever your style is, try to create an environment that allows for great conversation and a festive evening. You want everyone to be comfortable and feel the holiday spirit.”

When writer Olivia Noceda was a child, she would stay up late the night before Thanksgiving and design custom menus for everyone on Microsoft Paint, which she would then print out, frame, and place on each table setting in preparation for the big meal. The holiday was a big deal for Noceda’s mother, who would always put on the most beautiful Thanksgiving gatherings. “I think that's where my love for hosting and setting a beautiful table really started,” she explains.

However, for the last six years, Noceda has been having Thanksgiving with her husband’s side of the family, so when she does host around the holiday, it’s typically for Friendsgiving. “I don't necessarily pick a theme, but I do like everything to feel cohesive,” she explains of her decorating process. “I usually start incorporating little autumnal touches sometime in October and let them carry me through Thanksgiving.”

The writer and recipe developer lets the season guide her, building her tablescape and decor around a beautiful branch from the farmers’ market, a bowl of pears or persimmons, a collection of little pumpkins and squash, or a few taper candles on the dining room table. Ingredients play a big part in her decor—she can even take a fresh loaf of bread and make it feel special on her table.

For the rest of her decor, she likes to incorporate vintage serving pieces, everyday elements, and elevated details like antique silver and linen napkins with things she already owns to lean into that lived-in, warm feeling. The last thing she wants is her home to feel like a department store display. “Nothing needs to match perfectly, and I actually prefer when it doesn't,” Noceda adds.

What entrepreneur and Real Housewives of New York star Erin Lichy remembers best about Thanksgivings growing up is the smell. “We always spent Thanksgiving out east, and the house was full of pumpkins, local hay, and lanterns,” she explains. The scent of that fresh produce, mixed with her grandmother’s homemade turkey t’bit, has shaped the way she decorates for and hosts Thanksgiving. “That coveted and highly personal (and incredibly delicious) recipe is in my cookbook, She’s a Host, and it’s part of every Thanksgiving I host.”

Decorating starts in the kitchen for her family. Lichy wants the home to smell like fall, so she focuses on that sense, making seasonal recipes with squash and pumpkin and incorporating autumnal flowers in her interiors. The melding of those scents gives her townhouse a warm feeling, especially when mixed with layered fall colors and the crackling of the fireplace.

Ever since Lichy and her family have moved into their townhouse, she’s been obsessed with decorating her stoop too. “The stoop is the first thing everyone sees, so this year I decided to bring in Simon Wallace Design to make it really stand out!” Lichy adds. “They completely transformed it with layers of seasonal color and texture, and it feels so warm and welcoming.” She specifically loves using the faux garlands and pinecones, as well as lanterns, from Balsam Hill and Frontgate because they’re so beautifully made that they look real, and she’s able to reuse them year after year.

No matter what she does to decorate though, she just wants her home to feel warm and lived-in, and this is the season and holiday to really lean into that.

Decorating for the holidays is Gabby Amato’s version of a self-care day. “It's truly one of my favorite days of the year,” she says. “I carve out a weekend afternoon when my husband and son are out of the house, light my favorite fall candles, turn on some music, and have the house entirely to myself.”

Amato, the founder of Table Two Studio, a paper plate brand which specializes in beautiful, artistic designs that you definitely won’t want to throw away, credits her love of Thanksgiving and the fall season in general to her mother. While growing up in the ‘90s, she saw her mom put so much care and effort into the holiday decorations, always making their home feel warm, nostalgic, and full of spirit. “For Thanksgiving, she decorated our mantel with gourds, greenery, and ceramic pilgrim statues that were a gift from my grandmother,” Amato says. “We had oversized cornstalks and pumpkins at the front door. Nothing ever felt overly styled or precious.”

It’s this feeling that has stuck with her throughout her life, and it’s what she hopes she captures in her own home every year. She begins decorating for the fall early, right after Labor Day, pulling out old pieces and unboxing anything new and arranging it all in fresh ways so nothing feels stale. “I love beautiful decor, of course, but I'm much more interested in creating an atmosphere than achieving a particular aesthetic,” she adds. “I want my home to feel warm, lived-in, a little nostalgic, and festive.”

Donna Lennard’s family was never one for holiday dressing—most of the attention at Thanksgiving fell on the delicious food shared with family. As a restauranteur and cofounder of beloved NYC establishment il Buco, it makes sense why the meal remains to be the most important thing to Lennard even today.

“Clearly, the table is always festive, whether it’s the tablecloth, the napkins, or linens that reflect the season in burnt orange and the like,” she explains. “I love to decorate a table with assorted colorful gourds and seasonal florals, beautiful candlelight (our beeswax candles already have an orange glow), orange ranunculus, and the occasional dried floral.” Colorful Pienza glassware dots the table and her plethora of beautiful tableware gives it more character, but she keeps everything else relatively simple to ensure the food remains the center of attention.

Though Lennard doesn’t often host her family and friends for the Thanksgiving meal, she’s already looking forward to next year when everyone will finally gather at her beach house.

Being from Australia, Kendall Knox—the cofounder of Olive Ateliers along with her husband—didn’t have a personal reference of what the holiday “should” be or look like. This gives her the freedom of deciding what it is for her family. “What it has become is an intimate table: my husband Ben, our son Rhodes, and Ben's mum,” she says. “No rotation, no kids' table, no forty-pound bird. Just the four of us and a dinner that runs long because nobody is in a hurry to leave it.”

Since Knox isn’t beholden to the classic Thanksgiving decor, she tends to keep her home and her table rather simple, “shopping the house,” as she puts it to decorate instead of buying something new. She keeps a rather neutral palette of rich browns, creamy whites, terracotta, and silver, then uses antique linens, mismatched silverware, and candlesticks that have collected wax to capture the true lived-in feel.

Her focus tends to be on the table and the meal, letting the food add more color. If a tablecloth has a wine ring on it from the year before, all the better—it complements the stacked plates at the end of the meal.

“The objects that come out every year, that get a little more marked each time, are the ones that end up meaning the most,” Knox adds. “Thanksgiving is just a very good excuse to use them.”

#6832. There’s Science Behind Why You’re So Obsessed With Your Partner's Smell

Is it pheromones, chemistry, or something else entirely?

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

You know that thing where your partner's T-shirt somehow smells better after they've worn it? Or when you find yourself nuzzling into their neck during a hug because you genuinely can't get enough of how they smell? It's not necessarily their cologne, shampoo, or laundry detergent you're into, either. Sometimes, it's just them. And apparently, you're really into it.

If you've ever wondered why you're so attracted to your partner's natural smell, you've probably heard that pheromones are to blame. There's this idea that our bodies can somehow sniff out the people we're most compatible with, even before our brains catch up. But the science behind that theory isn't nearly as straightforward as you might think.

“Researchers haven't definitively identified a human sex pheromone that reliably produces sexual attraction in the way we sometimes see in other species,” explains Casey Tanner, a LELO sexpert and certified sex therapist.

That doesn't mean your partner's smell has nothing to do with your feelings for them, though. Our sense of smell is closely connected to memory and emotion, which helps explain why someone's scent can become so comforting (or downright irresistible) once we're attached to them. But does loving the way your partner smells actually mean you're compatible? And should you be concerned if you don't particularly care for their scent? Here's what the science says.

Pheromones are chemical signals released by one member of a species that can trigger specific behavioral or physical responses in another member of the same species. In the animal kingdom, they play a role in everything from attracting mates to marking territory. But when it comes to humans, things get a little less clear.

According to Tanner, while pheromones are well-documented in other animals, their role in human sexual attraction remains unproven. So, there's no solid scientific evidence that your body is sending out some irresistible chemical signal that makes certain people want to jump your bones.

And while pheromones and body odor often get lumped together, they're not actually the same thing. According to Tanner, your natural body odor is essentially your unique scent signature, shaped by everything from genetics and hormones to skin bacteria, diet, and your environment. Unlike pheromones, which are defined by the biological responses they trigger, ordinary body odor doesn't have to produce a specific response to influence how someone feels about you. So, while you might find someone's body odor incredibly attractive, that doesn't mean you're responding to pheromones.

Research suggests that body odor can influence attraction and who we're drawn to, but scientists are still figuring out exactly how much of a role it plays. And just because someone's natural scent appeals to you doesn't mean your bodies have identified each other as a perfect genetic match.

If you've ever found yourself lingering over your partner's pillow after they've left, there's a reason their scent can feel so comforting. And it has a lot to do with how closely our sense of smell is connected to memory and emotion.

“When we're falling for someone, their smell can become intertwined with experiences of pleasure, affection, comfort, and arousal,” Tanner explains. “Over time, that scent can become a kind of shortcut to feeling close to that person!”

Basically, your brain starts associating your partner's natural scent with all the things you love about being around them, from cuddling on the couch to having sex. And those associations can stick around even when you're physically apart.

“Once we’re attached to that person, their body odor essentially becomes a reinforcement or reward,” says psychologist Pamela Dalton, PhD, MPH. Their scent can bring back positive memories and feelings of intimacy, which helps explain why something as simple as a worn shirt can make you feel closer to someone you're missing.

As much as we'd love to tell you that being obsessed with your partner's natural scent means you've found your soulmate, science doesn't exactly back that up. Sure, there may be some biological factors at play, but liking the way someone smells isn't proof that you're genetically compatible, much less destined to spend your lives together.

Some research has explored whether people are more attracted to the body odor of potential partners whose immune-system genes differ from their own. The theory is that these differences could lead to greater immune diversity in biological offspring. But according to Tanner, the findings have been inconsistent, and there's no reliable evidence that humans choose romantic partners based on genetic compatibility.

And even if someone's scent makes you want to bury your face in their chest 24/7, that doesn't tell you much about whether you'll actually work as a couple.

“Loving your partner's scent can be a true reflection of attraction and attachment, but no, it’s not a reliable diagnostic tool for long-term compatibility,” Tanner says. “Chemistry is a great indicator of initial attraction, but compatibility is something we have to discover, negotiate, and create over time.”

In other words, enjoying someone's natural smell is a nice bonus, but it's not a substitute for shared values, trust, communication, or the other things that make a relationship work. And if you're not immediately drawn to someone's scent, that doesn't mean attraction can't develop as you get to know them.

If you've made it this far and realized you've never once felt the urge to steal your partner's T-shirt just to smell it, don't stress. Not everyone is particularly sensitive to scent, and being indifferent to your partner's natural smell doesn't mean you're any less attracted to them.

According to Tanner, people vary in how sensitive they are to smells, how much attention they pay to them, and how important scent is to their experience of attraction. Some people might be more drawn to their partner's appearance, voice, personality, or the emotional connection they share.

“If you’re indifferent to a person’s smell, it’s likely not going to be a game-changer because once you have the opportunity to get to know the person, you find [other] positives and compatibility between the two of you,” says Dalton.

And if you're otherwise happy with your relationship, there's no reason to start questioning it just because your partner's scent doesn't do anything special for you. As Tanner puts it, “If you’re into them, don’t go looking for a problem just because you haven’t noticed their scent!”

Okay, but what if you used to love your partner's natural scent and now you can't stand it? Before you start questioning your entire relationship, know that a change in how you perceive someone's smell doesn't automatically mean your feelings for them have changed.

According to Tanner, everything from hormonal fluctuations and medications to diet, illness, and changes in your sense of smell can affect how you experience your partner's scent. Pregnancy, for example, can make you more sensitive to certain smells. And sometimes, the explanation is as simple as your partner switching shampoos or laundry detergents.

Of course, emotions can play a role, too. Because scent is so closely connected to memory and feelings, your associations with someone's smell can change as your relationship evolves.

“There is evidence that when, for whatever reason, that relationship isn’t working, that body odor is no longer pleasant or preferred,” says Dalton. “All of a sudden, because of the association with negative characteristics, [the body odor] is changing. Your response is changing.”

Still, suddenly disliking your partner's scent isn't proof that something is wrong between you. As Tanner explains, a change in smell isn't a reliable indicator of relationship conflict, and there may be a physical explanation worth considering first.

If you notice a significant change in your partner's actual body odor, it's worth gently asking whether anything has changed with their health, medications, or routine. And if there's a particularly noticeable change in genital odor, Tanner recommends encouraging them to check in with a healthcare professional.

Bottom line: A change in how your partner smells to you isn't automatically a sign that something's wrong. How you feel about your partner's scent can change for all sorts of reasons, and it's just one small part of the bigger picture.

#6831. Ella Rubin on Her Banner Year: ‘It’s Out of Body in the Best Way’

The Sterling Point star shares her hopes for season 2 and the watch she’ll have “forever.”

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

It’s a good time to be Ella Rubin. The Sterling Point star has had the breakout year actors only dream of, after her role as Annie Jacobson in the hit Prime Video series launched her to viral heights. “It’s out of body in the best way,” she tells ELLE. “It’s a one-in-a-million chance to get to work as an actor, especially on a show that you love and you’re proud of.” Also on her agenda? An intimate holiday dinner with Chanel to celebrate its watches and fine jewelry.

On an unseasonably warm Tuesday night in New York, the French fashion house hosted ambassadors, friends, and more at Daniel Humm’s new, already hard-to-reserve restaurant, HUMM. As she got ready for her outing, the 25-year-old was as relaxed as ever. In a downtown hotel room, a scene unfolded that any 20-something woman would recognize: “Wide Open Spaces” by The Chicks played on a speaker (a song Rubin calls her “Annie anthem”), a light girl dinner of French fries and soda splayed across a table, and a friend on hand for moral support.

Rubin, an Upper West Side native herself, knows that for a Manhattan night on the town, sometimes less is more. The actress opted for a black tweed jacket—in true Gabrielle Chanel fashion—from look 40 of the Métiers d’art 2026 collection. Paired with a simple white tank and perfectly fitting Levi’s, “it’s my ideal dinner look,” she says.

In terms of accessories, Rubin wore a Chanel Première Ribbon watch, with diamonds elegantly placed around the face. “A beautiful gift,” she says, “and also something you’ll have forever, which is sort of the cornerstone of why I love Chanel jewelry so much, as it’s pieces you’ll know and love, and hopefully, give to someone someday.”

Despite not having her ears pierced, Rubin’s accessories game remains strong. The simplicity of Rubin’s outfit allowed for her jewels to truly shine as she finished off her look with stacked Chanel Fine Jewelry Coco Crush ear cuffs and rings in 18K white gold and diamonds. For glam, makeup artist Shayna Goldberg kept things natural and dewy, starting with a seamless base of Chanel Beauty Les Beiges Healthy Glow Foundation, topped off with the Les Beiges Healthy Glow Natural Eyeshadow Palette and Les Beiges Mascara. The finishing touch? Rouge Coco Longwear Satin Lipstick in 124 Marie and Le Crayon Lèvres Precision Lip-Defining Pencil in Nude Brun.

Below, Rubin reflects on her busy year, plots her holiday gifting list—Chanel included, of course—and reveals who she’d most like to guest star on season 2 of Sterling Point. Someone get Julia Roberts on the phone, stat!

I’m in a really lovely structured jacket, with this high kind of collar, and really beautiful buttons. They’re just so ornate and detailed. I find the jacket to be incredible, [and] my favorite kind of thing to wear. And then, I’m in just some Levi’s and a heel. Because of the high neck, we went with a ponytail, sort of like, a 60’s influence. And then the jewels, part of the reason for the ponytail is because I’m wearing some ear cuffs, which…I don’t actually have my ears pierced. So, I’m really glad that I’m getting to explore that side of jewelry. And then, I love a watch, and I love rings. Those are sort of my staples, my favorites.

It almost feels too late for me. I did it when I was a kid. And they closed up immediately, because I played soccer, and I just wasn’t committed. Now we’re just rocking with it, and it almost feels like, “Okay, this is just where we're at, and where we're staying.” But because Chanel makes such wonderful clip-ons, I get the opportunity to wear lovely earrings now.

Oh, my God. All of them. So many of them. Some of the cuffs I’m wearing tonight, honestly, luckily, they don’t need me to have my ears pierced for them, but if I had to, I would.

Something that’s comfortable, that feels like a part of you, and a part of your outfit. Something where you feel like yourself, it’s an extension of you.

There is this Coco Crush ring that I have now, very, very luckily. It’s on one side, just metal. And then, on the other side, it’s diamonds. And it’s just shiny and beautiful, and lovely, and it feels both, really simple and casual, and also, a party in the back.

That’s a great question. I think a watch is an incredible gift. I’ve had the pleasure of wearing a Chanel watch tonight, and on the show I’m on. It feels just like an incredible staple that, of course, also has utility, and it’s really classic and useful, and a beautiful gift.

It’s just so wild. It’s very surreal. It’s out of body in the best way, but also, I feel unexpectedly grounded, because I’m surrounded by wonderful people, but also, it means I get to work more, hopefully, and be more committed to the work I am doing, and feel more self-assured within it. So that's really the ultimate goal. I just feel unbelievably fortunate to be in the position to keep working. So, I guess what I make of it is just trying to really not let it get old in any way, and keep enjoying myself, and feeling as grateful and in my body as I possibly can, which I do. So it's pretty wonderful. It's solely wonderful, yeah. I’m just going to choose to keep enjoying it, and sharing it with the people I love.

Always Sex in the City, of course. I watch a lot of movies. I recently saw Wild Horse Nine. I think, for fall and winter, going to the theater is the best activity. It’s my favorite thing to do in the world, but especially as it’s getting colder, it’s just an incredibly cozy, wonderful time to go do that kind of thing. I’ve been in the theater almost every single day. Catch me at any AMC or Film Forum.

I’m obsessed with Alison Oliver. Maybe we could discover another sister, and it’s a biological sister, and it’s Annie’s sister. I’m just desperate to work with so many actors. So I’m thinking of Julia Roberts, Viola Davis. I’m naming all my favorite actors in the world. Oh, my God. Susannah Perkins. I just did God of the Woods with them. They’re fantastic. I would do anything to work with them again.

Get ready for more laughing and crying. Season two is still being figured out, so there's so much I don't even know, but I will say I think we're going to only become more intimately close with these characters, and know them more deeply.

#6821. Hailey Bieber and Bella Hadid Make a Case For “Coyote Chai” Colored Fur Coats

Chocolate brown’s cooler cousin has officially entered the chat.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Move over, chocolate brown: There's a new shade taking over the celebrity style scene, and it's honestly the perfect shade for transitioning from fall to winter. Enter what I’m coining Coyote Chai, the deliciously rich, multi-tonal brown fur coat trend that Bella Hadid and Hailey Bieber are making a serious case for right now.

If winter dressing had a moodboard, these two would be running it. And while their personal aesthetics might differ, they've seemingly landed on the same outerwear obsession: fluffy, statement-making coats in a gorgeous mix of earthy browns, caramel, taupe, and deep brown.

Bella stepped out in a fuzzy, sandy-brown jacket paired with dark-wash denim and heels, alongside a brown Coach purse. Meanwhile, Hailey took a similarly tonal approach in New York, wearing a cropped, textured fur-effect jacket with warm brown, honey, and gray undertones, styled with classic jeans and barely-there black heels.

While it feels like chocolate brown has been the star of the show, this shift into what I’m calling Coyote Chai feels like the rightful move as we head closer to winter, brightening the sartorial vibes as it gets gloomier outside. This shade of brown is the cooler, blended cousin of espresso and caramel shades. As a non-coffee drinker and chai latte warrior, this light brown shade blended with hints of darker spots is the perfect layering style coat to be bundled up in. And as Bieber and Hadid show, the coat makes the simplest of outfits all the more interesting.

Consider this your sign to grab a good coat for winter, and maybe make it in this fun Coyote Chai shade.

#6814. Harry Styles-Approved Designer Macy Grimshaw Just Won the 2026 H&M Design Award

Her off-kilter pieces have also been worn by Emma Corrin, Simone Ashley, and Anya Taylor-Joy.

Earlier this year, Macy Grimshaw presented her graduate collection from Central Saint Martins at London Fashion Week, styled by Harry Lambert, the mind behind the wardrobes of Emma Corrin, Alexander Skarsgård, and Harry Styles. Since then, the 25-year-old designer has continued to rise, dressing the likes of Anya Taylor-Joy, Simone Ashley, and several of Lambert’s clients—Corrin and Styles among them—in her semi-surrealist separates and top-notch leatherwork. Now, Grimshaw has a major accolade to cap off her standout year: the 2026 H&M Design Award.

At the presentation on Wednesday afternoon in London, Grimshaw was chosen among seven other finalists from top design universities across the globe: Chico Yu, Ennis Finnerty Mackay, Evelina Kryvopust, Fantin Delattre Pirenne, Jessica Lin, Macy Grimshaw, Yichun Liu, and Yvonne Schichtel. Grimshaw will receive a €150,000 ($164,655) endowment, a yearlong mentorship, and will have her collection produced and sold in selected H&M stores worldwide and online in October 2027. Each finalist was awarded a €10,000 ($10,980) endowment.

“She left me with desire and intrigue to see how she can actually develop the work,” said Beka Gvishiani, founder of Style Not Com, who joined H&M’s creative advisor Ann-Sofie Johansson, model Paloma Elsesser, and designer Ib Kamara on the jury. “For the moment, it’s very much the artist’s work,” he continued. “But she has ideas about how to make it wearable for daywear, because some of the pieces are great for an event or a performance.”

Gvishiani highlights the collection’s more experimental pieces, like a rusted, laser-cut mesh minidress ornamented with padlocks or a terracotta-hued molded bustier scattered with discarded cigarette butts. The scenography of the latter was inspired, naturally, by the brick wall where CSM students gather on smoke breaks.

But in between those more experimental moments, it’s easy to see why Grimshaw walked away with the prize, H&M’s first time awarding it since 2020. While fodder for celebrity placements and red carpets, her work also carries “hanger appeal,” clothes that are both wearable and appealing but also evoke something unexpected and slightly “off.” Like a texturized, scalloped suit jacket with a taut waist à la the “New Look” or the almost wet-looking crumpled silk slip dress done in a graphic, almost watercolor effect.

The line took cues from the designer’s college years in London (at 18, she moved there from Hong Kong to attend CSM) and speaks to her introspective way of working. “My whole practice is inspired by my grandmother having Alzheimer’s and trying to represent the fading of memories by using everyday objects and to recontextualize them,” Grimshaw told ELLE after the award was announced. “I want to remind us not to take the everyday for granted; that’s why a lot of my work is pencil shavings, cigarettes, locks.”

Next up, the designer will head to H&M’s home base of Stockholm, where she’ll get started on her collaboration with the brand. “This opportunity is going to be a massive help to make my work accessible to everyone,” she adds. “I’m very excited because this collection was not designed with the idea of commerciality. So I’m really interested to challenge myself and also work with other people to see how it can become that.”
